- Security: `None` when the server offers it, whatever the host row holds. Otherwise VNC Authentication, type 2, when the row holds a password: the 16-byte challenge is DES-encrypted in ECB with the password as the key, cut or zero-padded to 8 bytes with the bits of every byte reversed, the quirk every VNC client since the first carries. A 3.3 server naming type 2 gets the same exchange. `SecurityResult` is read after it under 3.3, 3.7 and 3.8 alike, and only a 3.8 failure carries a reason string. Apple's `RFB 003.889` greeting is answered as 3.8, which it takes.
- Two reason lines beside `auth unsupported`: `password needed`, the server offers type 2 and the row has none; `password rejected`, the server failed the response. `auth unsupported` keeps meaning neither type is on the list. Struck (2026-09-05): Apple's own types 30–36. Type 2 works with the one box the user ticks, and the Diffie-Hellman plus AES exchange behind type 30 buys the same screen for a page more of crypto.
- Host: a nullable `vnc_password` column on `hosts`, schema 13, added only to a database that already had the table. The form shows a `[ VNC PASSWORD ]` field, obscured, only while the `VNC` row is on; blank saves null. It lives on the row rather than in the keystore beside the ssh key: the key already grants the account the tunnel runs as, the password only opens a display on that machine's own loopback, and one column keeps the host a single value the form saves whole. Struck (2026-09-05): a per-host keystore entry — a store, a provider, a delete hook and a memory double, for one eight-character secret.
- On the user's Mac: System Settings → General → Sharing → Screen Sharing → ⓘ → "VNC viewers may control screen with password", the password set there. Remote Login stays as it is. The README says so in place of the old limit.
- Regression tests: the response to a known challenge under an 8-character password and under a 2-character one matches DES vectors computed outside the app; `[2]` with a password sends the response and reaches `ServerInit`; `[1, 2]` takes `None`; `[2]` with no password is `password needed`; a failed `SecurityResult` with a reason is `password rejected` carrying it; a 3.3 server naming type 2 is authenticated the same way; a host row from before the column upgrades with a null password and takes one; the form's field is present only with `VNC` on.
- Ownership: `lib/platform/vnc/`, `lib/data/`, `lib/features/settings/host_form_screen.dart`, `lib/features/screen/screen_controller.dart`, `lib/design/components/field_panel.dart`, README, tests. Version bumps with the build that ships it.

### 39.50 The shell is a sheet over the tab, dragged down to leave (user, 2026-09-05, three shots of X's in-app browser at three heights: "¿podemos hacer que las sesiones ssh estén en un modal como el de twitter? si no estoy mal moshi también tiene uno y me beneficiaría")
- 39.37 struck "the terminal as a bottom sheet" and 39.38 kept it struck, on the ground that a route plus back is the same gesture. The user has now asked for the sheet in their own words, the second time after 39.38's "que sea un modal sheet es útil", which is what this spec waits for. The ground was also thinner than it read: on the phone, back belongs to the keyboard while the keyboard is up, so leaving a shell mid-typing was two presses and a guess about which one came next. A sheet gives leaving a gesture of its own, the one Moshi has. Amends 39.36's "pushes `/ <HOST> SHELL`" and 39.38's "The sheet is not built"; lifts 39.37's strike.
- What it is: `openHostShell` presents the shell as a modal sheet over the screen it was opened from — the SSH tab, a host panel on HOSTS, a session card — and no longer as a page in front of it. The screen beneath stays drawn under a wash (`0x99000000`, lighter than the panel sheet's `0xCC` so the tab still reads as the tab), and a band of it shows above the sheet: the sheet's top edge sits 24 px under the status bar, with a hairline `borderNormal` along it. Nothing else says sheet: no handle, no rounded shoulders, no elevation, as 39.4's panel sheets. The band is the sign that the shell is over something; the header is the grip.
- Drag: a vertical drag that starts on the header row, or on the reason row under it, moves the sheet with the finger one to one, the wash thinning with it; terminal, key bar and keyboard go along. Released past a third of the way down, or flung downward faster than 700 px/s, the sheet finishes its way down and the route pops; released short of that, or flung upward, it comes back. The header's cells still take their taps, a tap and a drag being told apart by the arena as everywhere else. The terminal keeps its one-finger drag as the scroll and the key bar keeps its horizontal one; neither moves the sheet. A tap on the band leaves too, as a tap on a panel sheet's wash does. Back still leaves, and the last tab closing still pops, both down the same slide.
- Motion: the sheet rises over `Motion.normal` on `Motion.enter` and goes down on `Motion.exit`, and from a release it goes down from where the finger left it, not from the top; under the finger it is linear. Landscape stays allowed; the band costs the same 24 px there.
- The session lives on exactly as before. The sheet is the route and the store never was, so leaving by the drag is leaving by back: the tabs are where they were on the next open, and the `ACTIVE SESSIONS` cards are the way back in.
- Struck (2026-09-05): X's parked state, the sheet as one row at the bottom with the page behind it whole. It earns its place in X because the post's actions are worth having while the page is read; a shell parked as a header over the SSH tab is the card that tab already draws. One resting height.
- Regression tests: the shell opens over the screen it came from and that screen stays in the tree; the route is not opaque; the sheet's top edge sits 24 px under the status bar; a drag on the header past a third pops the route and the store keeps the session; a drag short of a third leaves the sheet in place; a downward fling pops; a tap on the band pops; the `+` cell under the grip still opens a tab.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart` and its tests. Version bumps with the build that ships it.

### 39.51 The sheet's band goes, and a rule on its top edge says it is a sheet (user, 2026-09-05, on 39.50: "me gusta pero hay un problema, no es facil de identificar que es un modal además de que desperdicia un poco el espacio hacia arriba, el espacio en una ssh view es algo crítico, tomalo como una nueva iteración")
- 39.50 spent 24 px on the band and asked the band to carry the whole sign. It carried neither well: what the band shows is the tab's own ground, near black under a wash, so the sheet read as a page that had lost some rows rather than as a layer over something. The rows are what a terminal is; a sign that costs rows and does not sign is the worst of both. Amends 39.50's band and its "no handle".
- What changes: the sheet stands flush under the status bar. Its height is the screen's less the status bar, the same rows the page had before 39.50, so the sheet costs the terminal nothing. In place of the band, a rule on the sheet's own top edge: 36 × 2 px, centred, `textDisabled`, sitting 5 px under the edge and drawn over the header's ground, with the hairline `borderNormal` still along the edge itself. It is the one thing that says sheet, and it says it in the row the header already holds, so it costs no rows either. It takes no touches: the header under it is still what the finger grabs, and the header's cells still take their taps.
- The wash stays `0x99000000` over the screen beneath, which is now seen only while the sheet is moving: a drag shows the tab behind it, which is where the layering reads loudest. 39.50's tap on the band goes with the band. What is left above the sheet is the status bar strip, and a tap there never reaches the app — the system takes it — so tap to leave is gone, verified on the emulator. The drag and back are the ways out, and the drag is the one the rule points at.
- Struck with the band (2026-09-05): the sheet as an inset card, held clear of the screen's edges on three sides. Columns cost more than the top ever did.
- Regression tests: the sheet's top edge sits at the status bar and its foot at the screen's, so the terminal keeps the rows a page had; the rule is on the sheet's edge, is 36 x 2, and a drag begun on it moves the sheet, so it is a sign and not a target; everything 39.50 lists still holds but its tap on the band, which is struck.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart` and its tests. Version bumps with the build that ships it.

### 39.52 The shell parks as a bar over the nav, one tap back into the sheet (user, 2026-09-05, on 39.50 and 39.51: "something else, i don't know what is the sense of having a sheet modal if there's no easy way to comeback")
- 39.50 struck X's parked state on the ground that "a shell parked as a header over the SSH tab is the card that tab already draws". That read the card from the wrong place. The card is on SSH and only on SSH: from HOME, PROJECTS or HOSTS the way back into a running shell is a tab switch and then a card, or a host panel and its `SHELL >` row — two or three taps to undo one drag, and the drag is what 39.50 made cheap. A way out that cheap needs a way in to match. Lifts 39.50's strike.
- What it is: one row, 36 px, over the nav bar and under all four tabs, drawn whenever any host has a session. Ground `surfacePrimary`, hairline `borderNormal` along its top, capped to the content column as the nav bar is. Left to right: the session's state dot, `<HOST> <n>`, the shell's own title in `rowKicker` when it has named itself, and `RESUME` in the accent at the right edge. A tap raises the sheet on that session by name, so the tab the bar reads is the tab that comes up.
- Which session it names: the tab last opened or brought forward, on the host it is on. The store already knows which tab each host is on, and this adds only which host that was; a restored run parks on the tab that was on screen when the process died. Closing the named tab falls to whatever its host is on now, closing a host's last tab falls to another host that still has one, and the bar goes with the last session anywhere.
- The bar stands under the sheet rather than appearing when the sheet leaves. Nothing animates it in or out: it is drawn the moment the first session opens, so it is already there before the drag starts and is drawn where the sheet lands the instant the sheet is gone. A row sliding in under the nav while a sheet is going down is two things moving in the same 180 ms. Struck as a claim (2026-09-05, verified on the emulator): the bar showing through mid-drag as the place the shell is going. The sheet slides as a whole and its foot goes down with its head, so the screen's bottom row is sheet until the sheet has left; what a drag reveals is the tab's top, under the wash.
- The cost is one row on the tabs, and only while a shell is running. 39.51 refused 24 px at the top of the terminal because rows are what a terminal is; this spends 36 px on screens that scroll.
- Struck (2026-09-05): a drag up from the bar that raises the sheet under the finger. The route does not exist until the push, so the gesture would drive a controller it made mid-drag, and on a row this size the tap is already the whole target. Struck: a close cell beside `RESUME`. Killing a shell belongs to the header row inside it, not to a 36 px bar the thumb passes over on its way to the nav.
- Regression tests: nothing open draws no bar; a session parks the bar directly over the nav bar reading `<HOST> <n>` and `RESUME`; a tap raises the shell it names; a sheet dragged down leaves the bar standing and a tap on it brings the sheet back; the bar follows the tab last brought forward across hosts; the last session closing takes the bar; a host whose last tab closed falls to a host that still has one.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/parked_shell_bar.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sessions.dart`, `lib/app/dazzle_app.dart` and their tests. Version bumps with the build that ships it.

### 39.53 The header's host name opens a switcher over every host's shells (user, 2026-09-05, on 39.52: "nice, it is excelent! something i am noticikg is that two hosts can't convive in our current harness, can you smth about it?")
- The chrome 39.50 through 39.52 built is one host wide. The sheet is pushed with a host and its tab row is that host's tabs; the parked bar names one session. A second host's shells exist in the store and are reachable nowhere from inside a shell: leaving the sheet, crossing to SSH or HOSTS and coming back in is the only route between them, which is the walk 39.52 just paid to remove for one host.
- What it is: the host name at the head of the header becomes a cell, marked with the `expand` glyph at `affordance` size in `textDisabled`. It is a cell only while a second host has a session, and a plain label otherwise, so nothing offers a switch that leads to one place. A tap opens a `SHELLS` panel sheet listing every session on every open host, host by host, each row `<HOST> <n>` with the shell's title under it and its state dot at the left; the row the sheet is showing carries `ON SCREEN`.
- Picking a row on the host in front is a tab switch, the same call the tab row makes. Picking a row on another host hands the sheet that host: the sheet keeps its route and rebuilds its contents under the new host's key, which runs the arriving host through the same path a fresh open takes, so it lands on the tab picked and its tab row is that host's. One route means one way out — the drag that was there before the switch is the drag that is there after it, and it goes to the tab underneath, not back to the host swapped away.
- The parked bar takes the count the switcher makes visible: `<HOST> <n>` then `+N` in the muted weight, where N is every other tab on every other host. The bar leads to one shell and this is how it says the others are running.
- Struck (2026-09-05): a second row of host chips over the tab row. The tab row is already the widest thing in a 36 px header and the second host is the exception, not the rule; a list that opens on demand costs nothing when one host is up.
- Regression tests: one host leaves the name a label; a second host makes it a cell, and the sheet lists every session across hosts with `ON SCREEN` on the one in front; picking another host's tab keeps the same route and shows that host, and the drag out still lands on the tab; picking a tab on the host in front activates it; the host switched to is the one the bar names; tabs behind the named one are counted.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart`, `lib/features/shell/parked_shell_bar.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sessions.dart` and their tests. Version bumps with the build that ships it.

### 39.54 The parked bar's verb reads TAP TO RESUME (user, 2026-09-05, on 39.52: "i think that it should say \"tap to resume\" instead")
- `RESUME` alone names what happens but not what does it. The bar is not a button with a border and it is not a row in a list; it is a strip that sits over the nav bar, and nothing else on it says the whole strip takes a tap. Amends 39.52's right-edge cell.
- What changes: the accent text at the right edge reads `TAP TO RESUME`. Same style, same place, same target — the whole bar, not the words.
- Regression tests: as 39.52, with the bar's verb read as `TAP TO RESUME`.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/parked_shell_bar.dart` and its test. No version bump: it ships with whatever build follows.

### 39.55 CODE: a registered directory is a primitive of its own, beside the projects (user, 2026-09-06: "quizá lo de projects deba de tener dos primitivos primarios, uno que sería los projects tipo linear, jira (como ahora) y otra la de los paths registrados que ya tenemos dentro de los hosts pero ya no se administraría desde ahí, tendría soporte para explorar tipo IDE, git diff (soportando worktree), invocar una terminal ssh ahí mismo también y más weas")
- A `paths` row is a host and a directory on it (39.35, narrowed by 39.45) and it is registered inside the host form, drawn under `[ INTEGRATIONS ]`. That places it wrong twice. A checkout is not a property of a machine the way an address and a port are — it outlives which machine holds it and it is what the user actually works in. And the row sits behind the `HERDR` toggle while the thing that finds it, `HerdrCommands.paths()`, is a `find` under `sh`: discovery needs sshd and nothing else. Two capability requirements on one row, and the stricter one is not the one the row is filed under. Amends 39.35's editor and 39.45's placement; 39.45's data model, its picker and its row rule all stand.
- **Two primitives, two homes.** PROJECTS is the 39.14 / 39.18 board and nothing else. CODE is every registered directory across every host, and it lives on HOSTS as the second segment of `[ MACHINES ]  [ CODE ]`, under the screen title and over the panel stack, because a registered directory is a host thing the app owns and not a Projects thing (the Code section). They are siblings, not a hierarchy: a workspace with no project is ordinary and a project with no workspace is ordinary. Struck (2026-09-06): a fifth destination. The nav is four labels wide inside a 600dp cap with 39.52's bar over it, and a switch that lives on the screen it switches costs no chrome at all.
- **The switch is the nav's own language.** Two cells, `rowKicker`, `textMuted` inactive and `accent` active, each under a full-cell rule of `BarSizes.inlineThickness` — accent on the active one, transparent on the other, which is the segment `_NavItem` already draws. Active means one thing in this app and this does not invent a second. The pick is remembered for the session, not persisted: HOSTS opens on MACHINES after a cold start.
- **A workspace row.** `<owner>/<repo>` in `rowTitle`, the 39.45 rule — the last two segments, which is what tells two checkouts of the same name apart — with the host name at the right edge in `rowKicker` `textMuted`. Under it, when the host has answered, the branch and the dirty count in the mono `rowSubtitle`: `main  +142 −38  4 files`, or `main` alone when clean. A host that has not answered yet draws the row without the line rather than a spinner per row.
- **Grouping is by host, ordering is by name.** One panel per host, titled with the host name, its count on the strip. A host with no registered directory draws no panel.
- **Registration leaves the host form.** `[ PATHS ]` goes. The CODE panel strip carries `ADD`, which asks which host first — a `PanelSheet` of the registered hosts, skipped when there is only one — and then opens `showPathPicker(context, host:, multiple: true)`, which already takes a host, already filters, already keeps registered rows out and already closes on `ADD n`. A long press on a row offers `RE-POINT` (the same sheet, single) and `REMOVE`. Nothing about the picker changes.
- **Discovery stops being a herdr thing.** The picker and the CODE list are drawn for any host the app can dial. `HERDR` still gates SEND, the agent marks and the AGENTS panel — starting a pane needs a running herdr session and always did (39.88: any running one, no longer one named `hub`). A host with sshd and no herdr gets the whole of 39.56 and 39.57 and no SEND row.
- **The link.** `paths.project_id`, nullable, referencing `projects` (schema v14, no backfill: every existing row is unlinked). A workspace belongs to at most one project; a project holds any number of workspaces. Set from either end — the workspace detail's `PROJECT` row, the project detail's `CODE` section — and cleared the same way. Deleting a project nulls the column rather than removing the row, in `DriftProjectRepository.delete` alongside the other Dart-side cleanups, because this database does not enable `PRAGMA foreign_keys`.
- **What the link buys.** 39.45 makes the target an every-send choice, which is right for a loose drop and wrong for the fifteenth item under the same project. SEND on an item whose project has exactly one workspace opens on that workspace preselected; more than one, its host's rows come first. The kind and the model are still asked — 39.45's ruling that those belong to the send, not to the directory, is untouched.
- Regression tests: HOSTS opens on MACHINES and the switch reaches CODE; CODE groups registered directories by host and reads `<owner>/<repo>` with the host at the right; the host form draws no `PATHS`; `ADD` asks the host and registers a multiple pick; a long press re-points and removes; a host with `HERDR` off still lists and still registers; linking a workspace to a project shows it on both screens and deleting the project leaves the workspace unlinked; SEND on an item under a project with one workspace opens preselected and still asks kind and model; a v13 database reads back with every path unlinked.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/projects/`, `lib/features/hosts/path_picker_sheet.dart`, `lib/features/settings/host_form_screen.dart`, `lib/data/`, `lib/application/`, and their tests. Version 1.0.0+53.

### 39.56 The workspace: its state, its worktrees, its diff, and a shell already at it (user, 2026-09-06, same message: "soporte para… git diff (soportando worktree), invocar una terminal ssh ahí mismo")
- **Everything on this screen is read back, not stored.** State that can be asked for again is not state worth a table — the same reason a dispatch row is written only when a pane id came back and `ShellTabs` keeps tabs to redial rather than sessions. 39.55 adds one nullable column and this section adds none.
- **One warm connection, one composed command per action.** `ShellConnection.exec` runs on a second channel of a connection this app already holds (built for 39.46's paste), so when a shell is open on the host the workspace pays one channel and no dial. When none is open the screen dials its own and keeps it for the screen's life. The budget is round trips: the header, the branch, the dirty count and the numstat are **one** `sh -c`, composed on the host, the way `HerdrCommands.paths()` is one pipeline and not three hundred stats. A screen that asks twice for one glance is the bug, not the milliseconds.
- **Header.** `git -C <dir> status --porcelain=v2 --branch` gives the branch, the upstream and the ahead/behind; `--porcelain` and not the human output, so a host in another locale answers the same. Detached reads as the short sha. A directory that is not a checkout says so in one line and keeps the shell row.
- **Worktrees are derived, never registered.** `git worktree list --porcelain` off the directory on screen. The detail draws a `WORKTREES` panel of the siblings — the last segment of each path and its branch — and a tap opens that sibling *as the workspace on screen*, without registering it, without a route push, the same swap 39.53 does when the shell sheet changes host. The row on screen carries `ON SCREEN`. Registration is unaffected: a worktree the user registers on purpose is another ordinary row, and one they never register is still reachable through its main checkout. `HerdrCommands.paths()` matches `.git` by name, so a linked worktree — whose `.git` is a file — is already offered by the picker; this is what makes those rows mean something instead of looking like duplicates.
- **Diff, in two round trips and not one.** `git -C <dir> diff --numstat` (and `--cached` behind a `STAGED` cell) is the list: one row per file, the path with its middle elided from the left, `+n −n` in `accent` and `warning`. The hunks come back with it rather than on tap, capped by bytes on the whole answer — a phone must never be handed a forty-file blob it will render twice on a rotate, and it is the cap that stops that rather than the tap. `--stat` is not used: it wraps by terminal width, which this screen does not have.
- **Reading a diff.** Mono `noteBody`, the old and new line numbers in a gutter, then one glyph — `+`, `−`, space — then the line. Added lines take `accent` over a tinted ground, removed take `danger` over theirs, context takes `textSecondary`, the `@@` header takes `textMuted` on `surfacePrimary` and the `diff --git` preamble is not drawn at all — the panel's own header already says which file this is. Struck (2026-09-09): no syntax colouring. The two colours that mean added and removed are the ground and the glyph, not the text between them, and the text between them is coloured by syntax — a second palette held under 6's, desaturated, spending neither the accent nor the danger red because both already mean something on this screen. Long lines scroll horizontally as a block, they do not wrap — a wrapped diff loses the column that makes it a diff. Binary says `BINARY`, and a file over the cap says how many lines it kept.
- **A shell already at it.** A `SHELL >` row, the host panel's row (`hosts_screen.dart`) with a directory behind it: it opens 39.50's sheet on that host and sends `cd <dir>` before the user's first keystroke, which is a dial the store already knows how to do. It joins the host's tab row like any other tab, 39.52's bar names it, 39.53's switcher lists it. `~` is passed through unexpanded — the expansion is the host's, as 39.35 already has it.
- Struck (2026-09-06): blame, staging cells, commit, push, and every other verb that writes. The workspace reads; what writes is the shell one row down, and it is one tap away. Struck: polling. The header refreshes on entry, on a pull, and when the screen comes back from the shell sheet — a phone in a pocket asking a laptop for `status` every five seconds is a battery bug wearing a feature's clothes.
- Regression tests: the header reads branch, upstream and ahead/behind off `--porcelain=v2`, and a detached head reads as a sha; a non-checkout says so and still offers the shell; the worktree panel lists the siblings with their branches and a tap swaps the workspace on screen without registering it; the numstat list renders and a tap fetches exactly one file's hunks; gutter colours and no wrapping; a binary file says `BINARY` and a huge file says what it kept; `SHELL >` opens a tab already at the directory and the parked bar names it; opening the screen while a shell is open on that host adds no dial; entry, pull and return from the sheet are the only refreshes.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/projects/workspace/`, `lib/platform/` (the command surface, beside `herdr_client.dart` and not inside it — none of these verbs is a herdr verb), `lib/features/shell/` for the seeded `cd`, and their tests. Version 1.0.0+54.

### 39.57 The explorer, and the inner display finally gets two panes (user, 2026-09-06: "file explore would be excelent in my case since i use a foldable phone")
- 39.17 capped every screen at 600dp and said "no two-pane layout until asked". This is the ask, and it is the right screen for it: a tree beside a file is the one layout that a near-square display serves better than a phone, and it is the reason the explorer is worth building at all.
- **Neither state is the fallback** (user, 2026-09-06: "i should be able to use it folded and unfolded!"). The outer display is where this phone spends most of its day and the explorer has to be whole there — not a cramped view of a layout that only works open. Both ship in the same slice; the folded path is written and tested first and the split is added over it, so the two-pane layout can never be the one that works and the single column the one that was left behind. A feature that only earns its keep unfolded does not land.
- **Folded, the same screen narrower.** The whole feature is here: the tree, the file, the diff, the shell row, every listing and every cap. The editor has the width and the tree is a drawer over it, as ruled under the explorer's sidebar and editor. Nothing is listed that has not been opened. This is deliberately the opposite of 39.18's rule for the project tree — that hierarchy is navigational and never drawn whole — because a file tree is the thing a user reads *as* a tree, and the reason to say so is that the two must not be built from one widget.
- **Unfolded, above 600dp, two panes.** Left, two fifths, the tree, expanding in place; right, the rest, the editor with its tabs. The scaffold's static and the nav bar keep 39.17's rules; the cap is lifted for this screen and no other. A fold mid-session narrows to the drawer layout with the tabs and the tree as they were; unfolding restores the split, under 39.17's proof (`cmd device_state state 2`, then `reset`).
- **One listing is one command.** `ls` with the type suffix, the size and the mtime for the whole directory in a single `sh -c` — never a stat per row — sorted directories first. Dotfiles are shown; a checkout's own `.git` is not. A listing is kept for the screen's life.
- **A file opens read-only, in 39.56's viewer.** Same mono, same horizontal scroll, a gutter of line numbers carrying the checkout's marks, and coloured whole by the app's own grammars. Capped by bytes, not lines, and the cap is said out loud when it bites. Binary is refused with its size and its type, not rendered. Struck (2026-09-06): editing, creating, renaming and deleting. The shell is one row away and it is better at all four; an editor here is a second, worse editor with no undo.
- **The explorer and the diff are the same right pane.** A file the diff list names opens with its hunks; the same file reached through the tree opens whole. One viewer, two feeds.
- Regression tests: folded, every verb of 39.56 and 39.57 is reachable and nothing is behind a width; folded, a tap opens a tab and closes the drawer over it; unfolded above 600dp, the tree is two fifths and a tap fills the editor beside it; folding keeps the open tabs and the open directories, unfolding restores the split; one listing is one command, once per directory; directories sort first and `.git` is hidden; a file opens read-only and a capped file says so; binary is refused; a diff row and a tree row of the same file open the same pane differently.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/projects/workspace/`, `lib/features/screen_scaffold.dart` for the lifted cap, and their tests. Version 1.0.0+55.

### 39.58 A press selects where the finger is, and takes the whole thing (user, 2026-09-06: "i have an annoying issue with the copy swlector, sometims happens that i hold press somewhere and it selects something like from 10 rows below. also i'd like more smarter automatic swlection when i indicate urls, words, etc")
- **The drift is temporal, not geometric.** A long press is reported half a second after the finger lands, and a drag frames later still. xterm resolved the pixel *then*, when the callback arrived — and a shell that is printing has scrolled the buffer under the held finger in the meantime, so the pixel now covers whatever text has since risen into it. Ten rows of output during the hold is a ten-row error, which is why it is "sometimes" and why it scales with how fast the shell is talking. The coordinate spaces were never wrong.
- **The press is resolved once, at pointer-down, as a cell.** A `Listener` above the terminal takes the down, turns it into a `CellAnchor` — the buffer's own handle on a line, which follows it through the scrollback — and every later callback reads the anchor back instead of a pixel. Only the *moving* end of a drag is still a live pixel, which is correct: that end is where the finger is now. One pointer at a time; a second finger is a pinch and must not move where the first one landed. An anchor whose line has fallen out of the scrollback answers nothing rather than a stale row.
- **The selection grips get the same treatment.** `_SelectionActionsState._held` — the end a grip is *not* dragging — is an anchor too, so output arriving mid-drag does not walk the still end up the screen.
- **The unit is the run, not the word.** `getWordBoundary` breaks on `.`, `:`, `-` and `/`, which are the characters a URL, a path and `file.dart:42` are made of, so it could only ever hand back one segment of them. The new rule is the run of non-blank cells around the finger, less the punctuation the run only happens to end in. Prose words are space-delimited, so this is a superset of what a word rule gets right, and it takes `/home/luis/x.dart:42`, `git@github.com:o/r.git` and `github.com/o/r` whole with no per-shape regex zoo.
- **A scheme inside the run wins the run**, which is what pulls the link out of `url=https://x.dev/a`. Off the link, the run is still the answer.
- **Trimming stops at the finger.** Sentence marks come off the tail, a wrapper around the whole of it comes off both ends, and a bracket whose other half is not in the run comes off — but nothing is ever peeled past where the press landed, so a press on the `.` of `done.` keeps it. `(bar)` inside a URL is balanced and stays; `foo).` is not and goes.
- **A soft wrap is not a boundary.** The logical line is walked out in both directions before it is read, so a URL that ran off the right edge is one token and one press.
- **A press on a gap still selects.** The rule answers null there and the caller falls back to the single cell, which is what keeps 39's existing drag-across-a-gap behaviour.
- Regression tests: output past the bottom of the view during the hold leaves the press on the word it landed on; a press inside `url=https://x.dev/a/b?c=1` takes the URL and a press on the prefix takes the run; `(done).` gives `done`; a URL wrapped across two rows is one press; a path, a `host:port` and an scp target survive their separators; a press on the trailing `.` keeps it; a press on a gap still takes the cell and the drag still follows the finger across the next one; output during the drag leaves the near end where the press was, and output past the scrollback's cap leaves a grip's still end on its own words; a second finger does not take the anchor off the first one's word; a press whose line is pushed out of the scrollback stops the drag rather than reading a row the buffer no longer answers for; a glyph wider than one cell does not slide the columns after it.
- Ownership: one session; `packages/xterm/lib/src/core/buffer/token.dart`, `buffer.dart`, `lib/src/ui/render.dart`, `lib/src/ui/gesture/gesture_handler.dart`, `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart`, `test/terminal_token_test.dart` and `test/shell_screen_test.dart`. Vendored xterm goes to `4.0.0+dazzle.3`. Version 1.0.0+55.

### 39.59 A modifier armed for more than one key: swipe it down to `CTRL(x2)` (user, 2026-09-06: "we have the ctrl in the toolbar, right? problem is that sometimes like with claude code i need to press Ctrl d Ctrl d fast to exit successfully, I'd like to be able to do the gesture to move it down once and it rotates with animation and says Ctrl(x2) so the next action can be done twice")
- **The one-shot rule was never wrong, it was only ever one.** 39.37 armed a modifier for exactly one key because a soft keyboard cannot hold two keys at once. The chords that end a program are not one key: `CTRL D` twice leaves a REPL and an agent, `CTRL C` twice stops what ignored the first. Under one-shot that is four taps with a re-arm between them, and the re-arm is the tap that gets missed.
- **The count is a gesture, not a second cell.** A downward drag on `CTRL` or `ALT` arms it and sets the count to two; another takes it to three, which is the cap. An upward drag walks it back down, and past two it disarms. A tap is unchanged: it arms for one key and disarms. No cell is added to a bar the phone already scrolls, and a user who never drags never sees a count.
- **The label rolls to say so.** The cell reads `CTRL(x2)`, and the change is a 140ms downward roll of the label — the old word leaves through the floor, the new one comes in through the ceiling. It is the same accent an armed modifier already draws in; the count is what is new, not the colour.
- **The count belongs to the armed set, not to a cell.** `CTRL` and `ALT` armed together at two apply to the next two keys, both of them. One session holds one arming: which modifiers, and how many keys they survive. Switching tabs switches it with the session, as the set already did.
- **One spender.** The armed set is spent where every byte passes — the store, as it already claims — and the bar stops clearing it on the side. Each send takes one off the count and the set clears when it runs out, so the bar's own keys and the characters the IME types straight into the terminal count the same. A reconnect, a close and a dead shell drop the count with the set.
- **Only a key spends it.** A terminal answers the program on its own behalf — where the cursor is, what it is, how big it is, where a finger touched — and xterm sent all of it out through the same callback the user's characters go through. An arming applied to those: `ALT` put an `ESC` in front of a cursor report and the program read the wrong answer, and every reply took one off the count, so an agent that asks where the cursor is left `CTRL(x2)` at one before a key was pressed. That is the `CTRL D` that did not exit. The fork grows `onReport` beside `onOutput` and the store writes it straight through: nothing armed applies to it, nothing armed is spent by it.
- Regression tests: a downward drag on `CTRL` arms it at two and the cell reads `CTRL(x2)`; a second drag reads `(x3)` and a third does not go past it; an upward drag walks back to two and then disarms; a tap on a counted cell disarms it whole; a horizontal drag on the bar still scrolls it and arms nothing; `CTRL` at two turns two typed `d`s into `04` `04` and the third into `64`; the count survives one key of a two-key arming across a tab switch; a bar key spends one of the count and no longer clears the set by itself; a reconnect drops the count with the set; a cursor report, the device attributes, the operating status, the size and a mouse report each leave the count where it was and go out unprefixed with `ALT` armed.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/design/components/key_cell.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_bar.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sessions.dart`, `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart`, `packages/xterm/lib/src/terminal.dart`, `test/key_cell_test.dart`, `test/shell_bar_test.dart`, `test/shell_sessions_test.dart`, `test/shell_screen_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+57.

### 39.60 A link is entered in a field of its own; a link inside the text stays text (user, 2026-09-06: "links must be a 1st class citizen, they can be embed in a drop text but normally, it should be provided by the user in other input like attachments", and on what an inline URL becomes: "if they wanna add it as attachment it should be in the separated input, if it is embeded in the main text then it should not get converted into an attschment but user can tap and get reodrected to it")
- **The link glyph stopped asking and started guessing.** 39.9 gave it two silent branches off invisible state: a clipboard that parses is attached with no prompt, and one that does not takes the field the user was writing in. Both were wrong in the same way — the tap does not say what it is about to do. The reported case is the first branch: an ssh host address copied for a shell became `https://100.118.60.77` as a chip, because `normalizeUrl` accepts a bare host and nothing stood between the clipboard and the row. The clipboard is a good guess and a bad decision.
- **The link gets its own line, over the strip.** Tapping `link` raises a one-line field between the chips and the glyph row, labelled `LINK`, url keyboard, prefilled with the clipboard's URL when it holds one and empty when it does not. The common case stays one tap and a confirm; the wrong clipboard is now visible and editable before it is anything. `ATTACH` commits it as a chip and closes the line; `x` closes it with nothing attached. This overrides 39.9's "never a second field": that rule was written against a *second body field*, and a line that belongs to the keyboard strip and dies with it is not one.
- **The capture field is never taken.** URL mode, the stash and the swapped hint are gone from both surfaces. The title stays on screen with its caret where it was while the link is typed above it, which is the whole point: the two are different things and were never one field's business.
- **`ATTACH` is dim until it parses.** A URL that `normalizeUrl` refuses used to vanish on submit with no word said. The block is inert and drawn in disabled ink until the line is a link, so the field itself is the answer and there is still no dialog.
- **A URL typed into the text is text.** Submit no longer extracts it, no longer strips it, and writes no attachment for it. The title is what was typed, always. The painted token stays and changes what it promises: it says *this is a link*, not *this will be filed*. One line reading `https://x.com/a` is a drop titled with that URL, not an untitled drop with a link — the user typed a sentence and the sentence is the item.
- **A painted token opens.** In the quick view's title readout the token is tappable and hands the URL to the browser; a tap anywhere else in the title still opens the editor. The list keeps whole-row tap: a row is a way into the item, and a finger landing on a URL must not leave the app.
- **The row says it holds links.** An item with url attachments carries a link mark at its trailing edge, the glyph with a count when there is more than one. It marks attachments only — an inline URL is already legible in the title and does not need announcing twice. It sits beside the `NEXT` arrow and the `SENT` tag rather than competing with them: those are states of the item and this is a property of it, so 39.6's precedence between the two is untouched.
- **The reason field rides along.** `BLOCKED BY` was the other user of the stash-and-hijack, and with a labelled line on the strip it uses the same one. The `_stashed` title parking leaves the codebase entirely rather than surviving for one caller.
- Struck (2026-09-06, replaced by this section): the clipboard attaching without a prompt; the field switching to URL mode; a title losing its URL when it would otherwise be empty; an inline URL producing an attachment row.
- Regression tests: the link glyph raises a labelled line and attaches nothing by itself; a clipboard URL arrives in that line as text and only `ATTACH` turns it into a chip; a clipboard holding prose leaves the line empty; the capture text and its caret survive the line opening, committing and cancelling; `ATTACH` is inert until the line parses and a refused URL keeps the line open; a URL typed into the capture line is submitted as the title with no attachment row; a line that is only a URL is titled by that URL and still writes nothing; the highlight still paints exactly the tokens it painted before; a tap on a painted token in the quick view opens it and a tap off it opens the editor; a row with one url attachment draws the mark and two draw the count; an inline URL draws no mark; the mark and the `SENT` tag share the trailing edge; the block reason is typed on the same line and still lands.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/design/components/keyboard_toolbar.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/link_input.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/link_text.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/link_mark.dart`, `lib/features/home/capture_bar.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/item_sheet.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/task_row.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/drops_panel.dart`, `lib/data/repositories/attachment_repository.dart`, `lib/application/providers.dart`, and their tests. Version 1.0.0+58.

### 39.61 A row knows its own index, so a press selects the row under the finger (user, 2026-09-06: "i tried to copy the url and instead the thing below was selected, don't know if it is because of a long buffer but this happens sometimes but not always so it is unstable selection", "to clarify, i hold press to select and something from rows below gets selected")
- **39.58 fixed the clock and not the ruler.** Anchoring the press at pointer-down removed the drift that comes from time passing during the hold, and it was real. What was left is a second, independent error with the same shape: a *brand-new* anchor, made the instant the finger lands, already names the wrong row. The offset is not the output that arrived during the press — it is the number of rows the view had shrunk by since the tab was opened, which is why it is stable within a session and different between them.
- **The scrollback ring never told its rows they had moved.** `IndexAwareCircularBuffer.trimStart` dropped items off the front by walking the start pointer forward and leaving the absolute origin where it was. Every surviving line then reported the index it had *before* the trim — `count` rows below where it now sits — and `createAnchorFromOffset` reads that index straight back. A press on row 3 asked for row 3 and was handed the line 12 rows further down.
- **A full-screen application trims on every layout change.** Claude Code's REPL runs on the alternate screen, which by definition keeps no scrollback, so `Terminal.resize` clears it on every resize; and because the shrink path walks the cursor up rather than popping when the cursor is on the last row, there is always something to clear. The soft keyboard coming up, the link line appearing, a pinch on the type — each one trims, each trim adds its rows to the error. On the normal screen the same call is a no-op, which is why the shell prompt was fine and the agent was not.
- **A shift must not tear a line out of the buffer it is still in.** The ring detached whatever it was about to overwrite, but a shift writes each slot from the one beside it: `scrollUp` runs `lines[i] = lines[i + 1]`, so the line just adopted at `i` is still sitting in slot `i + 1` when that slot is written next, and detaching it killed a line that had only moved. On the alternate screen every scroll goes through there, so a long press during output selected nothing at all — the other half of "sometimes".
- **A reflow starts the list over at the first slot.** `replaceWith` adopted through the old start pointer and only then reset it, so once the scrollback had wrapped, any width change rotated the whole buffer and the view came back showing lines from somewhere else. Same defect, same file, reachable by a pinch.
- **The rule, stated once: a row's index is where it is now.** Anything that moves rows — trimming the front, shifting a region, replacing the contents — moves their indices with them, and detaches only what is genuinely leaving. An anchor is only as good as the index it reads, and 39.58's guarantee rests on this one.
- Regression tests: a view that shrinks on the alternate screen leaves every row's index equal to its slot and a press three rows down takes the third row; a scroll leaves the rows it moved attached and walks an anchor up with its line; erasing the scrollback keeps the anchored row; a reflow after the buffer has wrapped keeps the order; and end to end, a press in a full-screen application after the keyboard has been up and gone takes the line under the finger.
- Ownership: one session; `packages/xterm/lib/src/utils/circular_buffer.dart`, `test/terminal_buffer_test.dart`, `test/shell_screen_test.dart`. Vendored xterm goes to `4.0.0+dazzle.5`. Version 1.0.0+59.

### 39.62 A session lets go of what it owns (user, 2026-09-06, on a session's controllers never being disposed: "do smth about it")
- **A tab that closes takes its own furniture with it.** A session owns three listenables the terminal view is handed rather than makes — the selection controller, the focus node and the scroll controller — and owning them is the whole point: they outlive the view so a tab switch keeps its selection, its caret and its place in the scrollback. The view disposes only what it made itself, so nothing ever disposed these. Closing and opening tabs through a long session left one of each behind every time, each still attached to the focus manager.
- **The furniture goes after the frame that removed the view, not before it.** Dropping the tab is what makes the route rebuild without it, and the rebuild is a frame away: until it runs, a live `Scrollable` is still attached to the scroll controller, and a controller disposed under it fails the detach it is about to be asked for. So the session is retired at the end of the frame the tab was dropped in, when the view holding it has gone.
- **Retiring is once and for all.** A session that has been retired is not reopened, reactivated or written to; a second retire does nothing. The terminal itself is left to go with the session — its scrollback is the largest thing a tab holds, and the only reference to it was the tab.
- Regression tests: closing a tab disposes its controller, its focus node and its scroll controller once the frame has run; closing one tab leaves another tab's alone; a shell that exits clean disposes its own the same way; the route drawing the closed tab detaches without raising; retiring twice is a no-op.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/shell_sessions.dart`, `test/shell_sessions_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+60.

### 39.63 A dial holds a claim on its tab (user, 2026-09-06, on the attempt counter guarding reconnects: "make it safe")
- **What may write to a session is whatever holds its claim, and nothing else.** Every dial takes the tab when it starts and writes state onto the session only while it still has it. A retry, the reconnect schedule and the tab closing each take the claim for themselves, which is what makes the dial they replaced go quiet: the one it holds is superseded from that moment and never becomes current again. A bare counter said the same thing in arithmetic, and arithmetic is the wrong shape — one session's number compares equal to another's, and a passing integer carries no trace of the tab it belongs to.
- **A closed tab never dials.** Closing takes the claim away for good rather than for now. Without that, closing a tab in the window a reconnect spends shutting the dead connection down was undone by the dial that followed: it took the next number, made itself current again, and opened a connection against a tab that was gone from the list — a live shell on the far host with nothing left in the app to close it.
- **A dial that lands after it lost the tab closes what it opened.** The connection is shut and dropped rather than kept, so a retry never leaves two shells running against one host in one tab.
- Regression tests: a tab closed while a reconnect is shutting the old connection down opens no new one and does not come up live; a dial that lands after the tab was closed closes what it opened; `STOP` and a retry each silence the dial they replaced; closing during the wait before a reconnect leaves the schedule dead.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/shell_sessions.dart`, `test/shell_sessions_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+61.

### 39.64 A change reaches the tab it happened to (user, 2026-09-06, on one notifier standing for every session: "refactor it then")
- **A session tells the views drawing it, and nothing else.** What a shell prints, what it calls itself, what state it is in, what its bar has armed and which clipboard its host turned out to have are all facts about one tab. Each is announced by that session. A key pressed with a modifier armed used to announce itself to the whole store, and the store is watched by the card strip, the host rows and the parked bar: every keystroke of a `CTRL` chord repainted every thumbnail on the screen behind the sheet.
- **The list tells the list.** Which tabs exist, which one a host is looking at, and which one is parked are facts about the set rather than about a tab, so those stay with the store: opening, closing, restoring and parking are what it announces, and they are the only things that are.
- **A view that draws one tab listens to that tab.** The card, its state dot, the parked bar and the sheet's own header each watch the session they name; a host row watches every session it counts. Nothing reads a session's state through a listener on the store, because the store no longer reports it.
- **A retired session announces nothing.** A change that arrives after the tab has let go of what it owns is dropped rather than delivered, so nothing that was in flight when a tab closed can reach a listener that has gone.
- Regression tests: arming a modifier and spending it tell the session and leave the store silent; a title, a state and a clipboard answer do the same; opening, closing and parking tell the store; a keystroke in one tab does not rebuild another tab's card; a change announced after retiring reaches nobody.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/shell_sessions.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_card.dart`, `lib/features/shell/parked_shell_bar.dart`, `lib/features/ssh/ssh_screen.dart`, `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart`, `test/shell_sessions_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+62.

### 39.65 The selection asks a surface where the cells are (user, 2026-09-06, on the shell route being one file: "you can even create an interface so every impl follows that")
- **What the selection needs from the thing under it is named, and named once.** A grip sits on a cell, the `COPY` cell sits over the top line of the range, and a grip dragged across the screen has to land on the cell under the finger. That is four questions — how tall a line is, how big a cell is, where a cell is on the screen, and which cell a point falls in — and they are the whole of what the selection layer knows about the terminal. They are a surface, and whatever draws the buffer answers them.
- **A cell's offset is already in the layer's own coordinates.** The terminal is drawn inside an inset and the overlay is not, so the two disagree about the origin. The surface settles it: what it answers is where the overlay must draw, with the inset carried in. Nothing on top of it adds a padding of its own.
- **No surface is not a wrong surface.** Between a tab being swapped and its view being laid out there is nothing to ask. The grips go off the screen and the `COPY` cell goes to the corner over the key bar, where a thumb already is — an answer, rather than a guess at coordinates nobody has.
- **The route is one job per file.** The sheet and its drag, the header, the terminal and its theme, and the selection are four things, and the file that holds the route holds the route. A rule drawn between two cells is one widget, not one per bar that wants it.
- Regression tests: a fake surface places the grips and the `COPY` cell where a terminal does; a surface reports a cell's offset with the terminal's inset already in it; with no surface the cell lands in the corner and the grips leave the screen; the route's own behaviour — tabs, the exit sheet, the pinch, the copy — is unchanged by the split.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_surface.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_selection.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sheet.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_header.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_terminal.dart`, `lib/design/components/cell_divider.dart`, `test/shell_selection_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+63.

### 39.66 A failure keeps the words it failed with (user, 2026-09-06, on the reason the transport had being thrown away: "do smth about it")
- **The line says what happened; the detail says what the machine said.** `key rejected` is the whole of what the header has room for and the whole of what most failures need. Underneath it is the transport's own sentence — the ssh error, the code the host answered with — and that is the one thing that tells the user which of the six ways a key can be refused this was. For a machine that did not answer the detail is the app's own, naming the dial, as *The detail under a machine that did not answer names the machine that was dialled* has it. It is carried on the state, not dropped at the seam that worded the line.
- **An error nobody mapped is its own detail.** A throw the transport has no reason for reads as a closed stream, which says nothing. Its words are kept as the detail, so the one failure the app cannot name is the one failure whose text is always there.
- **The reason row opens.** A tap on it shows the detail under it, wrapped, and a second tap puts it away. With no detail the row is not a button and does not answer a tap. What is open is copied by the cell beside it, because an error is something the user pastes somewhere else.
- **A reconnect does not carry a detail forward.** What is shown belongs to the failure on screen. A tab that gave up and a tab that failed outright each show their own, and a tab that came back shows none.
- **Closing a dead connection is a failure nobody needs told twice.** A dial that failed closes what it opened, and closing a socket the host reset fails with the reset. That close was started and not waited on, so its error had nowhere to go: every unreachable host put an unhandled exception in the log beside the failure the app had already worded. The close is awaited and its error dropped, so the log holds the one line the app wrote.
- Regression tests: a failure carries the transport's detail onto the state; an unmapped throw keeps its own words; the reason row opens on a tap, closes on a second, and does nothing without a detail; the open detail copies to the clipboard; a dial that fails leaves no unhandled error behind.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/shell_sessions.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_header.dart`, `lib/platform/shell/shell_tunnel.dart`, `test/shell_sessions_test.dart`, `test/shell_screen_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+64.

### 39.67 The key bar keeps its last cell and shows that it goes on (user, 2026-09-06, on `ATTACH` being off the right edge of the bar: "you're so damn right and it is an important tool!")
- **`ATTACH` is pinned, not scrolled.** On the phone the bar's cells are wider than the row, and the cell that puts a photo on the host sat past the right edge of a row that looks like a whole row: the only way in for a picture was a horizontal drag nothing asked for. It moves to the other side of the rule beside the keyboard toggle, where it is always under the thumb. Amends 39.46's cell and 39.37's bar, which pinned the keyboard toggle alone.
- **A row that goes on says so.** Whichever end of the scrolling half still has cells behind it is drawn over with a short fade into the bar's own surface, so a cell is cut by a gradient rather than by an edge that looks like the end of the row. A bar whose keys all fit fades at neither end, and the fades take no taps: the cell under one is pressed through it.
- **Nothing new is added to the bar.** The phone's text clipboard reaches the terminal through the IME and 39.46's ruling stands — there is no `PASTE` cell. A bar that hides its own cells is not fixed by putting another cell on it.
- Regression tests: `ATTACH` keeps its place while the keys scroll and still opens the sheet; the end fade is drawn with keys behind the right edge and the start fade once they are scrolled past; a bar wide enough for every cell draws neither; a tap lands on the cell under a fade; there is still no `PASTE` cell.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/shell_bar.dart`, `test/shell_bar_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+65.

### 39.68 A tab says which shell it is and how it is doing (user, 2026-09-06, on the tabs being bare integers: "i was thinking the same we should make it more reliable and stronger, i mean the tabs")
- **A tab is named by the shell in it.** A number says which tab was opened first and nothing else, and the one shell whose name is on the screen is the one already open. A tab takes the title its shell set, shortened for the room a tab has: the `user@host:` a login shell puts in front is dropped, a remainder that is one path is cut to its last segment, and what is left is drawn in the shell's own case and cut off by the width of the cell. A shell that has not named itself keeps its number.
- **The number is still what the app calls it.** The card, the switcher and the parked bar all read `NYX 2`, and a tab's key is its number. What changes is the word on the cell, not the session's name.
- **A tab carries its state.** The same dot the cards carry, in front of the word: a tab that dropped, gave up or exited is seen without being visited. It is the one thing on a tab that changes with nobody touching it.
- **The tab in use is on the screen.** The strip scrolls, and a tab going active — by a tap, by being opened, or by the one beside it closing — is brought into view. A strip that scrolled the shell the user is typing in off its own edge is how a tab is lost.
- Regression tests: a shell with no title reads its number and one with a title reads the title; `luis@nyx: ~/Projects/dazzle` reads `dazzle` and a title with spaces in it is kept whole; a long title cannot push the tabs past their share of the row; every tab carries a state dot and a dropped tab's dot differs from a live one's; a tab that says only its number is no wider than a tap target; opening a ninth tab brings it into view.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/shell_header.dart`, `test/shell_screen_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+66.

### 39.69 A card shows the tail of its shell, at a size that can be read (user, 2026-09-06, on the session cards being illegible: "yeah, do smth about it")
- **The card draws the bottom of the buffer, not all of it.** A whole viewport squeezed into a card put every glyph at three points wide: a grey texture that says a shell printed something and nothing about what. The miniature draws its lines at a fixed readable size and keeps the last ones that fit, so what a card shows is the newest output and the prompt under it. The rest falls off the top, which is where a terminal keeps what has already been read.
- **The rows below the last written line are not drawn.** A shell's viewport is mostly empty and a card anchored on its last row is a card of nothing. The blank rows after the last line with anything on it are dropped before the tail is taken; a blank line between two written ones is output and is kept.
- **A line wider than the card is cut by its edge.** The size of the text is what makes it readable, so nothing is scaled to make a full line fit. What runs past the card's edge is clipped there, the way the terminal itself clips.
- **The state dot leaves the first line alone.** The mark moves to the card's top trailing corner: the corner it sat in is where output now starts.
- Regression tests: the lines end at the last written row and a blank line between two written ones survives; a card's height decides how many lines it draws and the last one drawn is the newest; a line too wide for the card does not paint past it; the dot is in the trailing corner.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/shell_thumbnail.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_card.dart`, `test/shell_thumbnail_test.dart`, `test/ssh_route_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+67.

### 39.70 A view off the live line says so and offers the way back (user, 2026-09-06, on there being no way to the bottom of a shell: "yeah, we should implement it")
- **The terminal follows its output only while it is on the last line.** Scrolled up it stays where the reader put it and the shell goes on printing underneath, which is right and is the whole problem: nothing on the screen says the shell has moved on, and the way back is a drag of unknown length through a scrollback ten thousand lines deep. A cell over the terminal's bottom trailing corner appears the moment there is anything below the fold and goes when there is not.
- **A tap on it lands on the last line.** The view moves to the end of the buffer and the terminal takes the output back over: from there the shell scrolls itself again and the cell has nothing to offer.
- **A key is not a read.** Bytes from the bar go straight to the remote and never pass the view, so a shell asked for something while the reader was up in the scrollback answered off the screen. Anything sent to the shell puts the view back on the last line first.
- **One thing floats over the terminal at a time.** While there is a selection the corner belongs to the copy cell, and the way back waits until the selection is let go.
- Regression tests: no cell with the last line on the screen and one once the view is dragged off it; a tap on it puts the view on the last line and takes the cell away; output printed while the view is scrolled up moves neither the view nor the cell; a key from the bar puts the view back on the last line; no cell while there is a selection.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/shell_tail.dart`, `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sessions.dart`, `test/shell_screen_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+68.

### 39.71 The scrollback is searched from a line under the terminal (user, 2026-09-06, on a shell with no way to look for anything in it: "yeah, we should implement it")
- **`FIND` opens a line, not a page.** The bar takes one more cell, at the head of the keys where the row rests, and it raises a line between the terminal and the bar: a field, the count, a step either way and the way out. Amends 39.67, which refused the bar a cell for a paste the IME already does — a shell has no key that searches its own scrollback, and ten thousand lines is not a thing a thumb reads through.
- **What is typed is looked for and nothing is sent.** The line takes the keyboard off the terminal while it is up, so the characters land in the field; the buffer is read and the shell is not written to. The bar's keys still reach the shell, because a search is not a mode.
- **Every hit is marked and the one being read is marked apart.** Both are washes the text shows through, for the same reason the selection is one: a block over a glyph hides the very word that was found.
- **A search starts on the newest hit.** What is being looked for is almost always what just went past, so the first hit shown is the last one in the buffer and the steps walk back from there. The ends meet: past the oldest is the newest again.
- **The hit being read is put on the screen.** The view goes to the line the hit is on, which is what makes the mark worth drawing.
- **A hit rides the line it was found on.** The marks are anchored, so output printed under an open search leaves them on their own lines, and a line that falls out of the scrollback takes its mark with it.
- **A search stops at five hundred hits.** Past that the query is not a search, and marking every line of a buffer costs the frame that draws it. The newest five hundred are the ones kept.
- **Closing takes the marks with it and leaves the view where it is.** The line that was found is the reason the search was made.
- Regression tests: a query finds its hits whatever the case and more than one on a line; the search opens on the newest hit and the steps walk to the oldest and round to the newest again; the hit being read is marked apart from the rest; the view moves to the line the hit is on; what is typed in the line is not sent to the shell while a key from the bar still reaches it; closing clears the marks; a query with more hits than the cap keeps the newest five hundred.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/shell_search.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_bar.dart`, `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart`, `test/shell_search_test.dart`, `test/shell_bar_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+69.

### 39.72 The switcher takes over a host that has no shell yet (user, 2026-09-06, on 39.53: "i should be able to take over multiples hosts without the need to leave the modal or creating other")
- **The sheet lists what is running and what could be.** 39.53's `SHELLS` sheet lists sessions, so a host with none is reachable only by the walk 39.53 was built to remove: out of the sheet, across to `SSH` or `HOSTS`, and in again. Under the sessions the sheet gains a rule, the header `TAKE OVER` and one row per host with no shell, in the order the host list keeps them, each `<HOST>` over `ssh, <user>` — the shape a host row has everywhere else — and no state dot, because there is no session to have a state.
- **Picking one is the open the `+` makes.** The sheet is handed the host with no tab named, and the arriving host opens its first shell the way a first visit does, prompt and all — the unknown-key sheet names the host being dialled, not the one left behind. One route still: the sheet swaps its contents and the way out is the drag it was.
- **The name is a cell whenever there is somewhere to go.** Amends 39.53's "only while a second host has a session": the cell is there while a second host exists at all. One host and nothing else on the list leaves a plain label, because a switcher over one host leads back to itself.
- **A host is on one list or the other.** A host with a shell is a row per session in the top list and is not offered again below; a host whose last tab closed goes back to the bottom one.
- Regression tests: a second host with no session makes the name a cell and puts that host under `TAKE OVER`; picking it opens a shell on it, shows it in the same sheet route and leaves the drag out intact; a host with a session is in the top list and not under `TAKE OVER`; one host and no others leaves the name a label.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sheet.dart`, `test/shell_host_switch_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+70.


### 39.73 The attach sheet explains itself on a `?` (user, 2026-09-06: "attach button modal should give the user a '?' advice he can know what is going on and use case i guess")
- **A `?` on the header strip, beside `CANCEL`.** The sheet of 39.46 is two verbs and a grid of thumbnails, and nothing on it says where a picture goes or why anyone would send one: a host is uploaded to, a clipboard is written, a keystroke is sent, and all of that happens behind `PHOTOS`. The strip's affordance becomes a row — `?` first, then `CANCEL` — and `?` is a toggle, not a route: the sheet is the only surface the answer is on.
- **The copy is off until it is asked for.** A sheet that explains itself every time is a sheet whose explanation is scrolled past. Opening it drops the copy in above `PHOTOS`, over its own rule, and the `?` goes accent for as long as it is open; a second tap closes it. Nothing else on the sheet moves: the verbs, the tiles and the status line keep their order, and reading the copy attaches nothing.
- **Three things are said and no more.** What ATTACH is for — a picture put in the hands of whatever is running in the shell, read off the clipboard rather than named at it; what `PHOTOS` does — the gallery, many at once, in the order picked, and the path typed at the cursor where the host has no clipboard tool; and what `HELD` is — the crops of 39.45, kept for the app run, a tap to send and `×` to drop. It is the sheet's own body copy, so it is the Sans of the action sheet and not another tracked mono label.
- Regression tests: the sheet opens with a `?` on the strip and no copy under it; a tap raises the copy above `PHOTOS` and a second tap takes it away; the copy names the clipboard, the gallery and what `HELD` holds; the `?` reads accent while the copy is open and muted while it is not; `CANCEL` still closes the sheet with the copy open; raising the copy sends nothing to the host.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/shell/attach_sheet.dart`, `test/attach_sheet_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+71.

### 39.74 A modifier held down stays down until it is tapped off (user, 2026-09-06, on 39.59: "cuando mantienes presionado CTRL este se queda permanente hasta el siguiente tap como en moshi")
- **A press is the third answer the cell has.** 39.59 gave the count a gesture because a chord is not one key; a session at a REPL is not a count either. Walking a directory tree with `CTRL n` and `CTRL p`, or stepping a pager, is a modifier that should stay down for as long as the hand is working, and there is no number that says "until I say otherwise". A long press on `CTRL` or `ALT` latches it. A tap on a latched cell lets it go — the tap the user asked for, and the same tap that arms an unlatched one.
- **A latched arming is spent by nothing.** The count walks down as keys go; a latch does not move. Every key and every character the IME types goes through the modifier until it is released, and the store's one spender takes nothing off it. What already drops an arming still drops this one: a reconnect, a close and a dead shell.
- **The label says forever rather than a number.** The cell reads `CTRL(∞)`, arriving on the same 140ms roll `CTRL(x2)` does, in the same accent an armed modifier already draws in. A latch replaces the count: latching a cell that read `(x2)` reads `(∞)`, and releasing it goes back to the bare word.
- **The latch belongs to the arming, as the count does.** One session holds one arming: which modifiers, how many keys they survive, and whether they survive keys at all. `CTRL` latched and `ALT` tapped on after it are both latched, both applied to everything typed; tapping either takes that one off, and the arming ends when the last modifier does. Switching tabs switches it with the session.
- **Down is nothing, up is out.** A downward drag on a latched cell does nothing — there is nothing above forever — and an upward drag releases it whole, the same as the tap, because up is how 39.59 already loosens an arming.
- **The screen's strip is not this.** 39.34's strip has no count and gains no latch: a framebuffer takes a modifier through a real key press and release, and the strip's one-shot rule stands.
- Regression tests: a long press on `CTRL` latches it and the cell reads `CTRL(∞)`; a tap on a latched cell releases it and the word goes bare; a long press on a cell reading `CTRL(x2)` reads `(∞)` and the count is gone; three typed `d`s under a latched `CTRL` all leave as `04` and the arming is still up; a bar key under a latch spends nothing; a downward drag on a latched cell leaves it latched; an upward drag releases it; `ALT` tapped under a latched `CTRL` is latched too and tapping `CTRL` off leaves `ALT` latched alone; a long press on `ESC` sends `ESC` and latches nothing; a reconnect drops the latch with the set.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/design/components/key_cell.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_bar.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sessions.dart`, `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart`, `test/shell_bar_test.dart`, `test/shell_sessions_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+72.

### 39.75 What a URL is, and how one is opened, are each written once (user, 2026-09-06, ahead of the terminal's open verb: "ensure that related components and logic is correctly refactored first if not then begin eith that with a refactor commit")
- **The grammar leaves the feature that first needed it.** `normalizeUrl`, `urlTokens`, `UrlToken` and the chip label sat in `features/tasks/link_input.dart` because a URL was first typed into a link line. They are read by the capture bar, by the highlight controller, by the quick view's readout — and, next, by a terminal selection, which has no business reaching across into another feature's private file for the answer. They move to `application/urls.dart`, beside the other text rules the whole app shares, and the file no longer carries the name of one widget that uses it.
- **Opening one is a thing the device does, not a thing a feature knows.** `Uri.tryParse` followed by `launchUrl` in `externalApplication` mode was written out twice, character for character, in the two places tasks hands a link over. It becomes one `openUrl` in `platform/browser.dart`, beside the gallery and the notifications, and it hands back whether the launch took rather than dropping that on the floor — a caller that wants to know can now ask.
- **Nothing about a link changes.** Same rule for what parses, same promotion of a bare host to `https`, same refusal of prose, same external launch. This is where the code lives and nothing else; the suite that already covers all of it is the proof, and only its imports move.
- Regression tests: the existing link suite passes untouched but for its imports — the field paints exactly the tokens it painted, a painted token in the readout opens, a link attachment row opens, the clipboard prefill still refuses prose, and `ATTACH` is still inert until the line parses.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/application/urls.dart`, `lib/platform/browser.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/item_sheet.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/link_text.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/url_highlight_controller.dart`, `lib/features/home/capture_bar.dart`, `test/item_sheet_test.dart`, `test/url_highlight_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+73.

### 39.76 A selected link opens from the terminal (user, 2026-09-06, on a screenshot of a URL selected over `COPY` and `CLEAR`: "i am selecting an url but i'd like to have another option in the actiosn there, one to just open and that's it")
- **A third verb, and only when there is one to have.** The floating cell of 39.63 reads `COPY` and `CLEAR`; between them, on a selection that is a link, it now reads `OPEN`, which hands the text to the browser the same way a painted token in a title does. It is drawn only when the selection parses, so the cell says what the selection is: the verb appearing is the app agreeing that those cells are a URL, and a drag of a grip one character too far takes it away again. `CLEAR` keeps the trailing edge, because it is the way out of the selection and the last thing a thumb should find by accident; `COPY` keeps the first, because it is the verb that is always there.
- **The scheme is the whole test.** A selection is a link when it says `http` or `https` in front of an authority, and nothing else is promoted into one: `min.io/docs` off a wrapped paragraph, `/home/luis/x.dart:42`, `git@github.com:o/r.git` and `mailto:` are all left as text. Nothing demands a dot in the host either — `http://localhost:3000` and `http://100.118.60.77:8080/` are what a dev server and a tailnet address actually print, and a terminal is where you meet them. This is the opposite trade to 39.60's prose rule, which promotes a bare host and refuses a dotless one, and both stay: a title is prose and a selection is what the user dragged a grip across.
- **Whitespace ends it.** Any blank inside the selection and there is no verb: two words are not a URL, and a range dragged over a whole hard-wrapped line carries the newline that says so. A soft wrap does not — the buffer stitches a wrapped line back into one string — so a URL that ran off the right edge is still one link and still one tap, which is the same guarantee 39.62 gives the press that selected it.
- **Opening spends the selection, unless nothing took it.** The highlight goes when the browser goes, the way it goes on a copy: the verb is done with those cells. A launch the device refused — no browser, an unknown scheme it changed its mind about — leaves the selection and its cell exactly where they were, so the words are still there to copy instead.
- **No second look at the buffer.** 39.62's run rule already pulls the URL out of `url=https://x.dev/a` and takes a wrapped one whole, so the press that made the selection did the finding. This reads the text the selection already stands for and asks one question of it; there is no scan of the screen for links, no underlining of what was never touched, and nothing new between the finger and the terminal.
