- Regression tests: a selected `http://100.118.60.77:8080/` draws `OPEN` and a tap hands exactly that text to the browser and clears the selection; a selected `pgbouncer.conf` draws `COPY` and `CLEAR` and no `OPEN`; a device that refuses the launch keeps the selection up; a dotless host with a scheme is a link and a dotted word without one is not; the grips, the cell's placement over the top line and the corner fallback with no surface are unchanged.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/application/urls.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_selection.dart`, `test/urls_test.dart`, `test/shell_selection_test.dart`, `test/support/platform_channels.dart`. Version 1.0.0+74.

### 39.77 A drop's images go with it (user, 2026-09-07, on 39.35's send carrying only URL attachments: "make it send images too")
- **What was attached is what is sent.** 39.60 gave a drop two kinds of attachment and 39.35's send took one of them: the URLs became lines under the title and the pictures stayed on the phone. A picture attached to a drop is part of what the user wrote down, so it goes with the drop, and the choice of what an agent sees stops being a property of the attachment's kind.
- **The bytes go first, on the same connection.** Each image lands under `/tmp/dazzle` on its own exec channel before the workspace is created, which is 39.45's upload and 39.45's directory, and it is now written once for both: `mkdir -p /tmp/dazzle && cat > /tmp/dazzle/<name>`, the file whole on stdin, exit 0. Before the workspace, because an upload that fails then leaves nothing to close; and outside the 45-second deadline of 39.35, because a photo on a phone's uplink is not the hang that deadline is there to catch. No sftp and no gate verb — 39.48 retired the gate, and a plain exec channel is what the app already opens.
- **The prompt names the host's path and says nothing else.** The path goes on its own line under the links, in the order the pictures were attached. 39.45 already settled that a path in the text is the documented way into both agents, and 39.35 already settled that nothing is wrapped around what the user typed, so an image is `/tmp/dazzle/<name>` and no sentence. The bytes are on the host before the string is read, so the path is never a promise.
- **The name is the file's own, held to what a name may carry.** An image attachment is stored as `attachments/<uuid>.<ext>` and its base name is what it lands under; anything outside `A-Za-z0-9._-` becomes `_` and a name that would start with a dot is prefixed, so nothing interpolated into a remote path can climb out of the directory. The file is not re-encoded and not resized: it is the file the user picked, and 39.46's PNG conversion is a clip's rule, not an attachment's.
- **Half a send is not a send.** An attachment whose file is gone fails as `image unreadable` before the dial, and an upload the host refused fails as `image upload failed` — a fifth refusal line beside 39.35's four. Neither writes a dispatch row and neither creates a workspace, so a drop is either sent whole or not sent.
- Regression tests: the prompt puts one host path per line under the links, and an image with no link still opens on the blank line; a base name is taken from the local path, sanitised and prefixed when it starts with a dot, and `../` cannot leave `/tmp/dazzle`; the upload command makes the directory and takes the file whole; every attached file is read once in order and its bytes are what the transport is handed; an unreadable attachment never dials and writes no row; the fifth refusal has a line of its own and the five are five different lines.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/platform/shell/remote_shell.dart`, `lib/platform/shell/host_clipboard.dart`, `lib/platform/herdr_client.dart`, `lib/application/send.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/item_sheet.dart`, `test/item_send_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+75.

### 39.78 A drop's text is taken to the clipboard (user, 2026-09-07: "no puedo copiar rápidamente los textos de los drops para tenerlos a mano")
- **Two places, because there are two speeds.** A drop is written down to be used somewhere else, and until now the only way out of the app was retyping it. The quick view gains a `COPY` block, first on the footer, ahead of `MOVE`; the selection bar of 39.13 gains a third verb, `Icons.content_copy_outlined`, before the move arrow and the trash, which takes every checked row in one tap. The batch is the fast path — a long press already opens the mode — and the block is for the drop that is open anyway.
- **What is copied is what was written.** One drop is its title as it was typed, then a blank line and its links one per line: 39.35's shape, arrived at for the same reason, which is that nothing may be wrapped around the user's own words. It is not `sendPrompt` and does not call it — a send also names the host paths its pictures landed under (39.77), and a path on somebody else's machine is not something a phone can paste. Image attachments are left behind for that same reason: their value is a path under the app's support directory and it means nothing outside the app.
- **A batch is the entries joined by a blank line, in the order the panel draws them.** The set is unordered and the panel is not, so the rows are read back off the panel's own stream — newest first on Home, the project's own order inside a project — and a clipboard that came out in the order of a hash set would be a different list every time. Drops with links are separated by the same blank line their own links sit behind; a batch is almost always plain titles, and a stronger separator would be punctuation the user did not write.
- **The verb says what it did, because nothing else can.** Copying changes nothing on screen: no row moves, no sheet closes, and Android's own paste chip is not the app answering. So the block reads `COPIED` for two seconds and the bar's count slot reads `N COPIED` for the same, then both go back. Any change to the set puts the count back at once — a notice saying `2 COPIED` over three checked rows would be reporting on a selection that no longer exists.
- **Copying spends nothing.** The move and the delete end selection mode because the rows they acted on left the panel; a copy leaves every one of them where it was, so the mode stays up with the same set checked. It is also often the first half of a move, and making the user re-check three rows to do the second half is the app charging for its own tidiness.
- Drops only, on both surfaces: multi-select is a drops mode already, and the ruling is about drops. A filed task's footer carries seven verbs and an eighth is not being added to it on a guess.
- Regression tests: one drop with links is title, blank line, links; a drop with none is the title alone; a batch is the panel's order and not the set's, joined by a blank line, whatever order the ids were checked in, inside a project as well as on Home; the bar's third verb writes every checked row and leaves the mode and the set exactly as they were; the count slot reads `N COPIED` and goes back after the window, and a toggle puts it back at once; the quick view block writes one drop and reads `COPIED`; an image attachment is never on the clipboard.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/application/clipboard_text.dart`, `lib/data/repositories/attachment_repository.dart`, `lib/design/components/flash_block.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/drop_actions.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/selection_bar.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/item_sheet.dart`, `test/clipboard_text_test.dart`, `test/selection_bar_test.dart`, `test/item_sheet_test.dart`, `test/project_detail_test.dart`, `test/support/platform_channels.dart`. Version 1.0.0+78.

### 39.79 A picture is capped on the way in, and its upload is bounded (user, 2026-09-07, on 39.77's send: "i want you to eval if the upload process is as fast and safe as it can")
- **The cap is where the picture is picked, not where it is sent.** 39.77 kept the file the user chose exactly as it was; a phone camera's frame is four thousand pixels across and tens of times the bytes an agent reads any better for. The gallery picker is now asked for 39.46's 2048-pixel long side, the same edge a photo already goes to a host at from the shell, and that edge is one constant for both ways a picture gets there. Capping on the way in rather than on the way out means the copy under the app's support directory is the small one too, for the whole life of the drop, and the shrink is paid once instead of on every send. Nothing is re-encoded by us: the picker hands back what it hands back, and 39.46's PNG conversion stays a clip's rule.
- **An upload that stops moving is given up on.** 39.77 put the bytes outside 39.35's forty-five seconds and gave them no window of their own, so a send could hold `SENDING` for as long as the link stayed nominally open — and nothing underneath closes one: the SSH keepalive pings on its own timer and swallows whatever comes back, so a stalled channel is indistinguishable from a slow one all the way down. Each image now carries a budget: fifteen seconds to open the channel, plus the time its own bytes need at twenty-four kilobytes a second. Sized to the payload because a single window cannot be both short enough to catch a stall and long enough to carry a photo.
- **Past the budget the channel is closed and the send refuses.** The exchange is abandoned and whatever it fails with afterwards is dropped — the caller has already been told it gave up, and one failure is the whole of what happened. The refusal is 39.77's `image upload failed`, unchanged: from the outside a host that would not take the file and a host that stopped taking it are the same answer, and no workspace exists yet in either case.
- Regression tests: the picker is asked for the 2048 edge and the copy it returns is what is stored; a bounded exchange past its window throws and a failure arriving after that reaches nobody; an exchange given no window is waited on whole; the budget is the opening cost at zero bytes, grows with the payload, and stays inside minutes for a payload no attachment reaches.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/application/clips.dart`, `lib/features/shell/photo_attach.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/image_input.dart`, `lib/platform/herdr_client.dart`, `test/herdr_client_test.dart`, `test/image_input_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+77.

### 39.80 A passphrase the host asks for is asked for on the phone (user, 2026-09-07: "when they wanna use ssh key for anything it needs to be unlocked so the tty buffer is broken, can we handle it in the app as "authorization required" and ask password?", "the idea is to handle it in both or the phone or the computer on demand in a modal i guess", "i want a supreme solution damn it, without needing to go to vnc even when the graphical session is active", "for that we should create and offer our own pinentry, right?")
- **The prompt was never the app's to fix alone.** gpg-agent holds the SSH key and asks for its passphrase on whichever tty it was last told about, through a pinentry that draws over whatever is running there. A TUI in a Dazzle tab that touches the key gets a curses box painted across it and the two fight for the same keys, and no terminal can stop that from its own side. So the host runs its own pinentry, `pinentry-gate`, which never touches the tty that asked: it puts a modal on the computer — a fullscreen terminal window on the user's graphical session, a reserved console otherwise — and, at the same moment, writes the request into every terminal that asked to be told. The first decision wins and the others withdraw. Dazzle is one of those terminals, and this ruling is the terminal's half.
- **A shell marks its pty the moment it is up.** On the tab's own connection, an exec channel writes the pid of whatever holds the tab's pty to `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/pinentry-gate/tty/pts-N`. The holder is the connection's own sshd session, which is the channel's parent, or the Mosh server the tab was started under, found by the tab's mark in its environment: a Mosh shell hangs off a server that let go of the ssh session that started it the moment it came up, so the channel's parent owns no pty then and the mark would name nothing. The mark is the pid rather than a token because a mark whose holder has ended is then stale by inspection and the pinentry drops it itself, and the pinentry takes an sshd session or a Mosh server as a holder and nothing else; the app never unmarks, not on close and not on a redial, which marks again on its own. A host without the pinentry gets a directory nobody reads, a host whose process table disagrees gets nothing, and the exec's failure is dropped unspoken either way.
- **The request is a private OSC and the answer is a fifo.** `ESC ] 7771 ; pin ; <id> ; <base64 json> BEL` carries what the agent said — mode, description, prompt, the error line of a failed try, its own labels for the two verbs — and `ESC ] 7771 ; done ; <id> BEL` says the request was settled elsewhere. The terminal already parses OSC and hands the ones it does not know to the session, so nothing scans the byte stream for anything. The answer goes on a second exec channel: `cat` into the fifo the id names, guarded by `[ -p ]` so that a request already gone leaves the passphrase in no regular file. It travels on the channel's stdin and never on a command line; a bare newline is the cancel. An id is `[a-f0-9]{16,32}` or the OSC is not a request.
- **The bell is the signal and the host is the source.** The OSC reaches a tab whole over ssh, but a Mosh shell's pty ends at a server that ships a screen and not a byte stream, and an OSC it does not know never leaves the host. So the pinentry rings the pty's bell after every OSC, request and `done` alike, and a tab that hears a bell — any bell, whoever rang it — asks the host on an exec channel what its pinentry holds open: one line per request, the id and the json the OSC would carry, a request being open while its fifo stands. What comes back is the tab's request, and nothing coming back withdraws the one it had; a request the tab already holds is not replaced by itself, which is what makes the OSC and the bell say the same thing once over ssh. Bells that land while the host is being asked fold into one more ask, so a program that beeps ten times costs one exec and not ten, and the pinentry takes the fifo and its json down before it rings `done`, so the tab that asks then finds nothing. Mosh rings once per frame it ships, so a request and its `done` inside one frame ring once, and a tab whose only ask landed while the request stood would hold it after the computer answered; so a tab holding a request asks again every five seconds, for exactly as long as it holds one, and takes the empty answer as the withdrawal. One request is one fifo, so an answer from one tab settles it on every tab of the host at once, and no second sheet is raised for what was just answered. An answer that comes back on a connection that is no longer the tab's is dropped.
- **The sheet reads `AUTHORIZATION REQUIRED`.** Under the header: which shell, what the agent said about the key in its own words, the error line in the danger colour when a try failed, an obscured field under the agent's prompt, then `CANCEL` and `UNLOCK`. A confirm carries no field and draws the agent's own labels on the two blocks; a message draws one. Return on the field is `UNLOCK`. One at a time, as 39.65's exit sheets are, and on the shell route only: a request that lands while the user is on Home has the computer's modal, and is put up when the route is entered if it is still open then.
- **Withdrawal is not cancel.** `done` takes the sheet down with nothing sent — the computer answered, the agent gave up, or the host's own window ran out under the ask. Swiping the sheet away is the phone declining to be the one that answers: nothing is sent, the request stays open on the computer, and the sheet does not come back for that id. `CANCEL` is a decision and cancels everywhere. A connection that drops or a shell that exits takes the request with it, because the fifo it would be answered on is on the other side of a link that is gone.
- **The tab asks once as it comes up, and the sheet names the tab on screen.** A request made while a tab had no link, before it opened or across a redial, rang no bell it could hear, so every tab lists what the pinentry holds open as it connects, after its mark. Under Mosh every tab of the host hears the same bell and holds the same request, one tab a moment before the rest; the sheet goes up for the tab on screen when it holds one, else for the first live tab that does, and its `SHELL N` follows every tab of the host, not the store alone, since a tab taking a request is that tab's own news: it names the tab on screen from the moment that tab holds the same request.
- Regression tests: a `pin` OSC becomes the tab's request with the description, prompt and error the agent sent, and a `done` for the same id clears it while one for another leaves it; a shell that comes up runs the mark on its connection with its tab in it, and so does one that came back; the mark names the tab's Mosh variable and takes nothing that is not a mark; a bell runs the open list on the connection and its answer becomes the tab's request, a line whose id is not one being skipped; a bell whose answer is empty withdraws the request; bells that land while the host is being asked fold into one more; a tab holding a request asks again after the recheck and keeps asking until the answer is empty, then stops; an answer on one tab clears the same request on the host's other tabs and sends nothing on them; `UNLOCK` takes down the sheet and leaves the route under it ask; the open list reads only json files with a fifo beside them and exits 0 on a host without the pinentry; an answer runs the fifo command with the request's id, carries the passphrase and its newline on stdin and nothing on the command line, and clears the request; a cancel carries the bare newline; an id that is not hex is not a request and nothing is run; a redial drops the request; on the route, a request raises `AUTHORIZATION REQUIRED` with the agent's words, `UNLOCK` sends what was typed, `CANCEL` sends the newline, a `done` pops the sheet with nothing sent, and a sheet swiped away sends nothing and stays away for that id; a shell that comes up marks its pty and then asks what is open, and a request already open is found as it comes up; the sheet names the tab on screen once it holds the same request.
- Ownership: one session; host side `pkgs/pinentry-gate` in the dotfiles; `lib/platform/shell/remote_shell.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sessions.dart`, `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart`, `test/shell_sessions_test.dart`, `test/shell_keys_test.dart`, `test/shell_screen_test.dart`, `test/clip_paste_test.dart`, `test/attach_sheet_test.dart`, `test/pinentry_mark_test.dart`, `README.md`. Version 1.0.0+79; the Mosh holder and the bell, 2026-09-16 (user: "We had a passphrase pin entry unlock in the mobile side but that integration is no longer working and don't know why"); the audit, 2026-09-16 (user: "After that you should audit it").

### 39.81 A passphrase the host asks for rings the shade (user, 2026-09-16: "I want even the type of notifications that github gives me of "authorization required" when that happens")
- **The shade says what the sheet says.** One notification per host, on a channel of its own — `Passphrase requests`, high importance, the channel's sound and vibration — so a blocked agent can be kept quiet and a key being asked for still heard. The title is `AUTHORIZATION REQUIRED` and the text is the host's name in capitals and what the agent said about the key, the prompt when it described nothing, the name alone when it said nothing at all. It is GitHub's line for a sign-in it wants approved, which is the shape the user asked for, and it reads the same in the shade as on the sheet.
- **Up while any tab of the host holds the request, down the moment it is settled.** Two tabs that hear the same request are one notification; a request with another id replaces it in place and sounds once more, because it is another ask. Answered on the phone, answered on the computer, given up on by the agent, lost with the link, dropped by a redial or gone with its tab — each takes it down, and it comes down exactly once. The store is what knows all of that, so the store tells the shade, through a seam the tests stand a recorder behind; what a notification looks like is the platform's.
- **The tap is the way to the sheet.** Tapping the notification opens that host's shell, taking the sheet over from another host's shell if one is up; the passphrase sheet is already standing there, and the notification goes with the tap; the sheet stays until the request is settled. A tap that starts the process is parked the way an alert's `OPEN` is and replayed once Home is on screen, on the host it named — a host deleted since opens nothing. There are no `YES`/`NO` here: a passphrase is typed, never tapped, and nothing has to work with the activity gone, because the tabs that hear the request live nowhere else.
- **The ids keep out of the alerts' way.** An alert id is 1000 plus thirty bits of its host and pane; a passphrase id starts above that region and folds the host to twenty-nine bits, so the two never collide and both stay inside the signed 32-bit id Android takes. The payload names the host and nothing else: the sheet has the request.
- Regression tests: a request on two tabs of one host raises one notification, `HOST · what the agent said`; `done` on one tab leaves it up and `done` on the last takes it down; an answer on either tab takes it down; the tab that held it closing takes it down; another request replaces it in place; the prompt stands in for an empty description; the ids never meet an alert id and stay inside 32 bits; the payload round-trips its host and nothing else decodes as one; a tap is routed to the host's open handler and answers nothing; a cold tap under the splash lands on that host's shell, and one naming a host that is gone lands nowhere; a shell asked for on another host takes the sheet over from the one up.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/application/pin_alerts.dart`, `lib/platform/pin_notifications.dart`, `lib/platform/alert_actions.dart`, `lib/app/alerts_boot.dart`, `lib/app/splash_route.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sessions.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sheet.dart`, `test/pin_alerts_test.dart`, `test/shell_sheet_test.dart`, `test/shell_sessions_test.dart`, `test/sprite_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+113.

### A passphrase ask grows old, and an answer that reached nobody says so (user, 2026-09-16: "the app pinentry prompted me and i put the password in there", "so it expired why the app did not let me know", "include expire handle before the user prompts and after, app should be aware")
- **The host never says how long it waits.** `pinentry-gate` holds a request for a window of its own and takes the agent's `SETTIMEOUT` as one of the things it ignores, so nothing on the wire carries a deadline: the payload is the agent's words and the open listing says a request stands, never for how much longer. When the window runs out the fifo and its json come down and the same `done` an answer on the computer sends is rung, so from the phone's side an ask that expired and one settled at the computer are one event and always were. What the phone can know is its own clock, so it stamps an ask the moment it first hears it and counts up from there; it never counts down to a deadline it would be inventing, and it never says an ask is over on a count alone.
- **An ask the phone has held five minutes stops presenting itself as certainly live.** The count is taken on the recheck that already keeps the ask alive: a tab holding a request asks the host every five seconds whether it still stands, and one that keeps coming back past five minutes is drawn from then on as one the host's own window may have run out under. `MAY HAVE EXPIRED` in warning sits over the field and the affirmative block loses its fill, because an ask that may be over is not something the sheet asks for any more. Both blocks stay tappable: the host listed the request as open a moment ago, and refusing the answer on a local count would be the phone's guess overruling the host's own word. The id does not change as it ages, so the sheet stays where it is and the shade is not rung a second time for the same ask. Five minutes is the pinentry's own default window, which the phone cannot read and does not claim is the one in force.
- **An answer that reached nobody is not swallowed.** The passphrase is written into the fifo only while the fifo is there, so what that command comes back with is the verdict: a failure is proof the ask was already down when the answer left the phone, that nothing was written and that nothing is waiting on it. A command that never comes back says the same thing more quietly, and it is the commoner half: a pinentry that died without taking its fifo with it leaves a fifo that still passes the guard, and the write into it then waits on a reader that is never coming. So the answer is given five seconds to land, and a write still going when they run out is read as an ask that is gone. The channel it was on is taken down with it, because closing the channel is the only thing that ends a command blocked on something that will never arrive, and a dead request must not hold the connection and the passphrase written into it open for the life of the session. The phone says so instead of letting the user believe they unlocked anything — `AUTHORIZATION EXPIRED` over the shell it was typed on, that the host had taken the ask down so nothing reached it, and that the ask comes again if the key is still wanted. A channel that never came back at all is the link going, which is a different thing and already has the route's own word on it, so it gets no second sheet.
- **The shade is left where it was.** A notification re-rung at five minutes would wake the phone to say the same thing less certainly than the first time, so the shade keeps the rule the request already gave it: up while a tab holds the request, down the moment it is settled. Age is something the reader sees when they come to answer, which is the sheet.
- Regression tests: an ask that keeps coming back past the stale window is redrawn as one that may be over, on the same recheck and without a second notification; one younger than the window is not; an answer whose fifo command fails comes back as expired and one that runs comes back as sent; an answer whose write never lands comes back as expired on its own window rather than waiting; an answer whose channel throws comes back as lost; on the route, a stale ask draws `MAY HAVE EXPIRED` and an unfilled affirmative block, and an answer the host had already taken down raises `AUTHORIZATION EXPIRED` over the shell it was typed on.

### A new shell does not inherit the modes of the one that died (user, 2026-09-16: "look, for some reason i can't scroll .-.", "i already had this issue and was fixed but now i am seeing it again, it is an unstable scroll", "i can't even touch the herdr icon to toggle the sidebar")
- **The session outliving its connection is the design, and the terminal goes with it.** A tab keeps its buffer, its selection and its place in the scrollback across a network drop, so the shell that comes back writes under the output of the one that died instead of into a blank screen. That means the terminal object is the same one, and so is everything it holds — including the modes a program asked it for. Mouse reporting is one of those, and the program that turned it on is gone.
- **One bit left set reads as a terminal that has died under the finger.** While the terminal still believes an application wants mouse input, a drag is forwarded to the shell as a wheel report and the buffer stays where it is, and a tap is answered by the terminal rather than by whatever it landed on. The shell that came back asked for none of it and ignores all of it, so nothing scrolls, nothing under the thumb answers, and the screen says nothing about why. It lasts as long as the session, which on a phone is until the app is killed, and it returns the next time the link drops under a full-screen application — which is what made it read as something intermittent rather than something caused.
- **A new shell is given a terminal in the state a new terminal is in.** Above the reconnect mark, mouse reporting is turned off in each of the modes an application can ask for it, along with the encoding it reports in and bracketed paste. The alternate screen goes the same way, so the prompt lands over the scrollback the dead shell left rather than on a screen with nothing behind it; it is left only when the terminal is actually on it, because leaving it otherwise restores a cursor that was never saved.
- **A pane is the exception, because the pane is the thing that survived.** Reattaching to a herdr pane finds the same application still running on the far end, so the modes it set are still true of it and resetting them would be the lie. The reset belongs to a shell that is new, which is every reconnect that is not a pane.
- Regression tests: a session left on the alternate screen with mouse reporting on comes back from a reconnect on the normal screen with reporting off; a pane that reattaches keeps the reporting its application set.

### The wheel under a full-screen application is one listener, and it was left on a dead position (user, 2026-09-16: "okay but scrolling does not work while the keyboard is up, it is like the input cursor predates it", "without need of your \"fix\" i restardtdd the app and it's working again... very weird...", "and now it is bugged again... wtf")
- **A drag over a full-screen application is a scrollable being read, not a buffer being moved.** An application that asks for mouse reporting keeps nothing to scroll into, so the view under it is frozen and a second scrollable is laid over it whose only purpose is to have a position that travels. A render object beneath that one listens to the position and turns each line of travel into a wheel report on the wire. That single listener is the entire path: nothing else forwards the drag, and a finger moving a position nobody is listening to does nothing at all.
- **The framework hands a scrollable a new position whenever anything it depends on changes, and the listener stayed on the old one.** A scrollable rebuilds its position every time its dependencies change, whatever it was that changed, detaching the one it had and disposing it a microtask later. The render object registered the scroll listener when it was attached to the tree and took it off when it was detached, but the path that hands it the replacement moved a different listener across — the layout mark — so the new position went unheard and the one that mattered was left on the position already thrown away. From then on the drag is silent, for the life of the widget. Killing the app is what built the widget again, which is why a restart fixed it and why it came back on its own afterwards.
- **What changes under a terminal is the theme and the screen, and neither is the shell's doing.** The tokens are rebuilt from the system palette into a theme object that is new each time, so the theme animation notifies the scrollables under it for the length of the transition; and a phone that folds moves the app between two screens with different pixel ratios, which is another of the things a scrollable reads. Both arrive as a dependency change on the scrollable that carries the wheel, and neither is anything the reader did to the shell — which is what made it read as a thing that happens by itself.
- **The keyboard is when it is found, not what causes it.** The insets a soft keyboard moves are not among the things that scrollable depends on: the position it is dragging is the same object before and after, and the reports a drag sends with the keyboard up are the same reports it sends with the keyboard down. What the keyboard does is take half the screen, which is the moment a reader reaches for the scroll and finds it already dead.
- Regression tests: a drag forwards wheel reports again after the theme over the terminal has changed, and again after the screen's pixel ratio has.

### A click names a row of the screen, not a row of the scrollback
- **A terminal holds two coordinate spaces and they are not the same one.** The buffer is the scrollback and the screen in one list, and a cell in it names a row that follows its line for as long as the line is kept. The screen is the rows drawn right now, and its first row sits at the buffer's row less however deep the scrollback has grown. A pixel under a finger resolves into the buffer, because that is the space a selection lives in: an anchor taken there holds the words the finger landed on while the shell keeps printing under it.
- **What crosses the wire is the other one.** An application asked for mouse reporting because it draws a screen, and the screen is all it has: it keeps no scrollback and has no row to name outside the one it painted. A report carrying a buffer row puts every press as many rows below the finger as the scrollback is deep, and that distance grows with the session instead of staying wrong by a constant, which is what makes it read as a tap landing on the neighbour of the thing under it.
- **So the two paths resolve a pixel differently, and the reporting one takes a second step.** A press, a release and a wheel report take the cell the pixel falls in and then subtract the screen's first row, clamped to the screen. A selection, a long press and a drag keep the buffer's row untouched. On the alternate screen the two agree, a screen with nothing behind it being its own buffer, which is why a full-screen application is the last place the difference shows.

### 39.81 An agent is an object the app asks, not a name it keeps testing (user, 2026-09-07, ahead of the shell's turn jumper: "i was thinking of a model whwre we have multiple \"classes\", for codex, for claude, etc so we can just call an intertace using the factory pattern")
- **What the two agents differ by was written in five places.** Which flags start one, which models it offers, the words of its first-run trust dialog and the keys that accept it, the keys that answer it when it is blocked, and which mark file is drawn for it: each one a `switch` on a bare string, each in a different file, and each with its own answer for a name that is neither. A third agent is not a class to write but five switches to find. They become one sealed `AgentKind` with a case per agent, and every one of those questions is asked of the object.
- **The factory is the only door from a name to a kind, and there are two of them because there are two situations.** `AgentKind.of` throws on a name that is not an agent, which is what the send path already did by hand before it built a command out of it. `AgentKind.lookup` answers null, which is what the panel and the alert already needed: herdr reports whatever name the pane is running and the app draws `?` over it and answers it the way most TUIs are answered. Neither is a `switch` at the call site.
- **A name is what crosses the wire and what is stored; the object is the app's own vocabulary.** The dispatch row keeps `claude` as text, herdr keeps reporting names, and the command strings still carry `--kind claude`. The conversion happens at those edges and once: everything between the sheet's picker and the command builder passes the kind itself, so a misspelled agent cannot be typed into the middle of the app.
- **Sealed, so a third agent is a compile error and not a silent default.** The switches that answer per kind are exhaustive over the sealed type, and adding a case to the file makes the analyzer name every place that has to answer for it. That is the whole reason this is a type and not a map of strings.
- **Nothing either agent does changes.** Same start flags, same model lists, same trust dialogs and their keys, same answer keys, same mark files, same order in the picker. The suite that covers all of it is the proof, and only its types move.
- Regression tests: the factory answers the two kinds and refuses a third, and the looking-up door answers null for it; each kind carries its own start flags, models, trust match and answer keys, and the flags a send builds are byte for byte what they were; a trust screen belonging to one agent is not matched for the other; an unknown name still draws `?` and still answers `enter`/`esc`; the mark file named by each kind exists in the bundle.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/application/agent_kind.dart`, `lib/application/alerts.dart`, `lib/application/send.dart`, `lib/application/path_scan.dart`, `lib/platform/herdr_client.dart`, `lib/features/home/agent_mark.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/item_sheet.dart`, `test/agent_kind_test.dart`, `test/herdr_client_test.dart`, `test/item_send_test.dart`, `test/agents_panel_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+81.

### 39.83 An agent's state is a type, and `done` stops disappearing (user, 2026-09-07, on the ranked pattern findings: "you cam go for all of them, use tdd and commit pee each i guess")
- **The bug is the pattern.** herdr classifies a pane's agent as `idle`, `working`, `blocked`, `done` or `unknown`, and the app carried that as a bare string it compared against literals in four places: the panel's active list (`working` or `blocked`), its idle count (`idle`), its blocked count, and two colour switches that both ended in a fallback arm. `done` is named in none of them, so an agent in it was on no list and in no count — it left the Home panel entirely. That is the most common resting state on a phone: herdr calls an agent `done` when its work finished while its tab was never seen in the focused UI, and from a phone the tab is never seen. The refactor and the fix are the same change, which is why it is one.
- **A sealed `AgentState`, and the wire word is one of its cases.** Five classes, four of them constant and named, the fifth carrying whatever word herdr sent that this build does not place. `AgentState.of` never returns null and never throws: a word it does not know becomes `UnknownState(word)`, which answers no to every question and prints itself. So the switches over it are exhaustive with no `_ =>` arm, and the day herdr grows a sixth state the analyzer names every place that has to decide about it — rather than a fallback quietly swallowing it, which is exactly how `done` was lost.
- **The screens ask the state, they do not read its name.** `active` is what earns a row on the panel — working, or blocked and waiting on the user. `resting` is what the footer counts as idle, and it is true of `done` as well as `idle`: work that finished unread is still an agent sitting at a prompt, and counting it nowhere was the defect. `blocked` is the question only the user can answer, and the alert path keeps asking the snapshot that and not the word. `UnknownState` claims none of the three, because no is not known to be true either — it is not on the active list, not in a count, and still one of the agents in the header's total.
- **What it looks like stays with the design, not with the state.** One switch, in `agent_mark.dart`, shared by the Home panel row and the `VIEW ALL` route row, so the two cannot disagree about what `done` is drawn in. It is `textPrimary` — brighter than an idle agent's `textMuted`, because work waiting to be read is the thing on that screen worth walking to.
- **The word still crosses the wire unchanged.** `agent_status` is parsed into a state and serialised back out of one; a state the app does not know round-trips as the word herdr sent. Nothing above the client reads `word` except a row's label.
- Regression tests: every classified word maps to its case and round-trips; a word off the wire is trimmed, lowered, and an unplaceable one becomes an unknown state carrying it; active is working and blocked only, resting is idle and done, blocked is blocked alone, and an unknown state claims nothing; a `done` agent and an `idle` one count as two idle rather than being lost; an agent whose word the app cannot place is still in the header's total; a done row is drawn brighter than an idle one and an unplaced word is drawn as itself.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/application/agent_state.dart`, `lib/platform/herdr_client.dart`, `lib/platform/agent_watcher.dart`, `lib/features/home/agent_mark.dart`, `lib/features/home/agents_panel.dart`, `lib/features/agents/agents_screen.dart`, `test/agent_state_test.dart`, `test/agents_panel_test.dart`, `test/agents_screen_test.dart`, `test/alerts_test.dart`, `test/herdr_client_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+83.

### 39.84 Why it broke is one sentence, wherever it broke (user, 2026-09-07, on the ranked pattern findings: "you cam go for all of them, use tdd and commit pee each i guess")
- **Seven copies of the same handful of words.** A failed connection is found by four layers with four failure types — the herdr client's, the shell's, the screen route's and the agent poll's — and each of them spelled the sentences out: `key rejected` was written seven times, `<host> unreachable` five, `host key changed` in a helper the other five called and in one that ignored it. The send path and the path scan carried the same six-arm switch twice, character for character. Nobody wrote a wrong one, but nothing stopped anyone: the shell's header word and the shell's reason row were two separate switches over the same enum, kept in step only by whoever remembered both.
- **One vocabulary, named rather than written.** `ReasonLine` holds every sentence once — the ssh five, the two host key lines, and the screen route's own four — and each carries whether it names the host it happened to. Two do, because a machine that did not answer is about a machine; the rest are about this connection and read the same wherever they are found, so a host handed to one of those is dropped rather than prefixed and no caller has to know which kind it is holding. The header's uppercase word is derived from the same constant, so the two forms of the shell's failure cannot say different things about it.
- **Each failure type answers what it is, and none of them says it in words.** The herdr and screen-route failures carry a line and word themselves from it; the shell's kind and the host key refusal map through an extension beside the enum; the poll's outcome names one per case. Five mappings remain because there are five domains, and that is the irreducible part; what went is the wording. Changing what `key rejected` reads as is now one edit and every layer follows.
- **The one line with a value in it stays a function.** `protocol N` is not a constant sentence, so it is `protocolLine` next to the vocabulary rather than in it, and the two places that report a version call it. The constant half is in the enum for the header form.
- **Struck (2026-09-07): folding `HOSTS`'s verify affordance into it.** `NO ROUTE` and `NO ANSWER` look like the same facts abbreviated, and they are not: that chip reports a probe of a machine, and it separates a socket that opens and stays silent from one that never opens — a tarpit against a dead host, which the reason lines deliberately do not distinguish. Two vocabularies, because there are two questions.
- Regression tests: only the two lines about reaching a machine name one, and a host handed to any other is dropped; the header word is the line uppercased for every line there is; the four layers answer the same sentence for a rejected key, an unreachable machine and a changed host key; the protocol line keeps its number in both places that report one and says `?` with none; a snapshot has no line; each screen-route line is unchanged.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/application/reason_lines.dart`, `lib/application/agents.dart`, `lib/application/path_scan.dart`, `lib/application/send.dart`, `lib/platform/herdr_client.dart`, `lib/platform/host_keys.dart`, `lib/platform/shell/shell_connection.dart`, `lib/platform/vnc/vnc_failure.dart`, `lib/features/code/workspace_controller.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sessions.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_header.dart`, `test/reason_lines_test.dart`, `test/host_keys_test.dart`, `test/shell_sessions_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+84.

### 39.85 A mode carries what it needs, so the impossible case stops being checked (user, 2026-09-07, on the ranked pattern findings: "you cam go for all of them, use tdd and commit pee each i guess")
- **Two state machines kept their invariants by hand.** The item sheet's `_Mode` is five bodies, and two of them are about a send: which workspace was picked and what the send to it is doing lived beside the mode as their own nullable fields. Nothing tied them together, so a dispatch body read `target != null` three times and `?? ''` twice for a case that cannot happen, and going back read the send state to decide where back is. The screen route is the same shape one field wide: `ScreenPhase` is three states and the reason line sat next to it, null in two of them, read with `?? ''` in both places that draw it.
- **The payload goes on the case that has one.** `_Dispatch` carries the target it was opened with and the send state it has reached, and there is no way to be in it without a target — the three null checks and the `SEND` title fallback are gone with the field. `ScreenFailed` carries its reason; connecting and live carry nothing, because there is nothing they could say.
- **The switches were already exhaustive; that was never the problem.** Both were enums and the analyser already checked every arm. What it could not check was the second field: a mode is legal, a reason is legal, and the two of them together being wrong was invisible to it. Making the illegal pairs unrepresentable is the whole of the change, and no arm was added or removed.
- **Nothing on either screen moves.** The same five sheet bodies in the same order, the same back behaviour from a dispatch that has sent and one that has not, the same three lines on the breadcrumb and the same `CONNECTING` under an image that has not arrived. The suites that cover both are the proof, and only their types move.
- Regression tests: a dial that has not answered is connecting and has no reason to read; a failure puts its worded line on the phase itself; a retry goes back to connecting and the line goes with it.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/screen/screen_controller.dart`, `lib/features/screen/host_screen.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/item_sheet.dart`, `test/screen_phase_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+85.

### 39.86 The encodings the screen offers are the ones it can read (user, 2026-09-07, on the ranked pattern findings: "you cam go for all of them, use tdd and commit pee each i guess")
- **Two lists, and nothing holding them together.** The client sent a preference order in `SetEncodings` — ZRLE, CopyRect, Raw, DesktopSize — and decoded a rect in a switch with one arm each, written in a different file. Offering an encoding with no arm ends the stream on the first rect of it, with `rect encoding N` as the line on the screen. Writing an arm for one never offered is code no server can reach. Neither is something the analyser can see, because both halves are legal on their own.
- **One registry, and the offer is derived from it.** Each encoding is an object that knows its wire number and how to read a rect of it, and the list the client sends is those numbers in the order the registry is in. An encoding added to the registry is offered; one that is not in it cannot be. The bytes file writes bytes and no longer decides what the app can read: it takes the list from the caller.
- **A rect is not decodable from its own bytes.** ZRLE inflates against one zlib stream for the whole connection, so a decoder is handed a target — the reader, the image, and that stream — rather than a byte list. The one pseudo-encoding that invalidates every coordinate the server has sent says so on the target, and the read loop asks for the whole screen next time, in place of the decoder reaching into a field of the client.
- **Nothing on the wire changes.** The same four numbers in the same order, the same bytes for every message, the same reason line for a rect the client never asked for. The encoding an unknown number now resolves to is nothing, and that is the only case the read loop still has to word itself.
- Regression tests: the offer is the registry and every number in it resolves back; an encoding that was never offered has no entry; a raw rect lands where its header put it; a copy rect moves what is already there; the size pseudo-rect resizes and marks the screen stale.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/platform/vnc/rect_encoding.dart`, `lib/platform/vnc/rfb_client.dart`, `lib/platform/vnc/rfb_messages.dart`, `test/rect_encoding_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+86.

### 39.87 A tap on an agent row lands in its pane (user, 2026-09-08, on the Home panel: "i should be able to tap one of the agents ans get automatically redirected to the ssh modal in the correct pane if already there, if not then we need to add anothr herdr space to have it, it is a veey convenient feature, research first")
- **A row is the way into its pane.** A row on the Home panel and a row on the `AGENTS` route open the shell sheet on the host the agent is on, in a tab attached to its pane. The nav moves to SSH under the sheet first: that is the tab the sheet belongs to, the parked bar and the session card are there when the sheet goes down, and Home's poll stops for the visit. The `AGENTS` route stays under the sheet, so leaving the shell returns to the list it was picked from.
- **The tab is found before it is made.** A shell tab remembers the pane it was opened on. A tap on an agent whose pane a tab of that host already holds activates that tab and opens nothing. Otherwise the app explains that another terminal view will be detached and requires `OPEN HERE`; cancel changes neither route nor tab state. Confirm opens a fresh tab with `env HERDR_SESSION='<session>' herdr agent attach '<pane>' --takeover` as the pty command. The takeover transfers only terminal ownership and never replaces or stops the agent. The pane id is checked against the same shape every other herdr command checks before it is quoted into the line. At nine tabs the tap lands on the host's last active tab.
- **The binding is tab state.** The Herdr session and pane pair survive reconnects and app restarts. A reconnect reruns the command already consented to; a process restore reconstructs a normal attach to the recorded pane. A clean detach ends that command and closes the tab, so no tab remains claiming it still owns a pane.
- Regression tests: normal attach remains non-takeover and explicit takeover adds only its flag; a pane or session that is not valid is refused; cancel changes neither route nor sessions; `OPEN HERE` opens the exact pane and moves the nav to SSH; a second tap on the same agent activates that tab without asking again or opening another connection; a panel row and a route row both hand the tap the host and the agent.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/features/agents/agent_shell.dart`, `lib/features/home/agents_panel.dart`, `lib/features/agents/agents_screen.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sessions.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sheet.dart`, `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart`, `lib/platform/herdr_client.dart`, `test/agent_shell_test.dart`, `test/shell_sessions_test.dart`, `test/agents_panel_test.dart`, `test/agents_screen_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+87.

### 39.88 No herdr session is the product's (user, 2026-09-08, on the `hub` constant: "hub is a personal dicisoon, if we expose this as a product then people will be forced we don't want that, fix things please"; earlier the same day: "the user could configure the main they will use i guess unless we list all so there's noghing to config, just to display")
- **A session name was one machine's habit.** Every herdr command the app built carried `HERDR_SESSION=hub`, so the AGENTS panel, the alerts, SEND and the attach all worked on exactly one host in the world: the one whose owner named a session that. A product cannot ask that of anyone. The choice between a setting and discovery is settled by the user's own words: list them all, and there is nothing to configure.
- **The list is a sweep.** One exec channel asks the host which sessions it has, then asks each running one for its agents. The frame the sweep prints is one line per running session — the session's name, a tab, that session's `herdr agent list` document — closed by the `herdr session list --json` document. The one-shot list is one sweep; the watch is the same sweep every two seconds on the channel it already held. Each agent is stamped with the session it was listed in, and the stamp travels with the snapshot: across the watcher's isolate hop, into the notification's payload, onto the shell tab the attach opens. A session that stopped between the roster and its own turn answers `server_not_running` and is skipped; a session the roster calls running that printed no line is the host's `session list` no longer reading the way the sweep reads it, and that is a protocol mismatch, not an empty panel.
- **Every other command names the session its pane is in.** Read, keys, wait, prompt, process-info, workspace close and the attach carry `HERDR_SESSION='<session>'` for the session the agent was listed under, never a name of the app's own. A session name is checked against `[A-Za-z0-9_.-]+` before it is quoted into a line, the way a pane id is. Model discovery carries no session: it never talked to a server. A shell tab is bound to a session and a pane together, because two sessions on one host can both own a `w1:p1`.
- **SEND goes where a start can go.** The session `herdr` itself calls default is the target when it is running. When it is not and exactly one session is, that one is: the machine has one place to put an agent and the app does not make the user say so. Two or more running and no default is a question the app refuses to guess at: `no default session` on the SEND row, beside `no herdr session` for a host running none. Both are refusals of the spawn, after the connection, in the same vocabulary as the others.
- **The route names a session only when there are two.** The `AGENTS` route groups a host's agents by session and then by workspace, and prints the session in the panel title only when the host's agents span more than one, the same rule it already applies to the host's name. The README stops naming a session.
- Regression tests: the list and the watch carry no session name and the watch sleeps two seconds; a sweep of two sessions stamps each agent with its own; a stopped session's `server_not_running` answer is skipped; a roster session that printed no line is a protocol mismatch; an empty roster is an empty frame; the spawn target is the running default, else the only running session, else refused as none or as no default; every per-pane command carries the session it was given and a name outside the shape is refused; the attach line carries the agent's session; a tab is found by session and pane, and the same pane id under another session is another tab; the alert payload round-trips the session; a route with agents in two sessions names the session in the panel title and one session does not.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/platform/herdr_client.dart`, `lib/platform/agent_watcher.dart`, `lib/platform/alert_actions.dart`, `lib/application/alerts.dart`, `lib/application/send.dart`, `lib/features/agents/agent_shell.dart`, `lib/features/agents/agents_screen.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sessions.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_sheet.dart`, `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart`, `README.md`, `test/herdr_client_test.dart`, `test/alerts_test.dart`, `test/agent_shell_test.dart`, `test/shell_sessions_test.dart`, `test/agents_screen_test.dart`, `test/item_send_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+88.

### 39.89 A send's pane keeps no shell history (user, 2026-09-08, on the drop send: "little issue with drop send feature, it writes the zsh or anything history that main user uses and that is wrong")
- **The start line is the app's, and nobody typed it.** `herdr agent start` types the agent's whole command at the pane's interactive shell prompt, so every send since 39.45 wrote `claude --settings '{"skipDangerousModePermissionPrompt":true}' --dangerously-skip-permissions --model <slug>` or `codex --yolo` into the history the user searches on their own machine — twenty-two lines of it on the host this was found on. A history is a record of what its owner did, and a dispatch the phone made is not that.
- **The workspace is created without one.** `workspace create` carries `--env 'HISTORY_IGNORE=*' --env 'HISTIGNORE=*'`, zsh's name and bash's, and the shell the pane launches saves nothing it is handed. Not `HISTFILE`: a generated rc assigns that outright a moment after the environment is read, so a value put there loses to the file the rc names, while neither ignore list is touched by an rc that does not know it exists. Checked on the user's own host — the same `agent start`, typed into a pane created that way and answered by Claude coming up on its trust dialog, left the history file at the line it opened on.
- **The attach is untouched.** 39.87's seed is one line typed into the user's own tab, and that section counts on finding it one line up in the history when a detach returns to the prompt. Only a pane the app opened for a send is a pane nobody typed in.
- Regression tests: the create line reaches herdr as `--env HISTORY_IGNORE=*` and `--env HISTIGNORE=*`, the pattern arriving unexpanded by the shell that ran the line.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/platform/herdr_client.dart`, `test/herdr_client_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+89.

### 39.90 One field, capped, and the line break is a glyph (user, 2026-09-08: "props input are not consistent, there should be an specialized componetnt...?", "currently i can write like a lot and it doesn't get bigger while creating but this happens in edits, it should have a max cap", "\"enters\" are allowed but at the same time i like the fact that i have the \"check\" in the keyboard")
- **The field was written eight times, so it had eight answers.** The 2px accent caret, the dead underline, the dense box and the hint ink were copied into every input in the app, and the behaviour drifted with them: capture was pinned to one line and pushed what was typed off its own right edge, the quick view's title was unbounded and grew until it carried the sheet away, a form field capped itself but swapped the keyboard's key for a `↵` on the way, and the searches and the prompt each answered again. `DazzleField` is the only `TextField` left; every surface asks it for the skin and states its own line count, keyboard type and action beside it.
- **Six lines, then the field scrolls itself.** The body fields — capture, and the title the quick view edits — take the same ceiling. One line is the 36px row it always was; past six the text scrolls inside the field, so a long item neither hides its own start off the right edge nor takes the sheet it was typed in. The submit glyph on the capture line stays a 36px target beside a field that now has a height of its own.
- **The enter key is the commit, so the break is on the strip.** A keyboard draws one thing in that corner: an action, or a newline. `TextInputAction.done` over `TextInputType.text` is what puts the checkmark there, and a body field keeps both even at six lines — a multiline type asks for the `↵` in the same place and takes the check with it. So `newline` joins the default row on both surfaces, `link`, `image`, `↵`, `+`, which is the one thing 39.10's "nothing else is visible" now admits. It puts a break at the caret, and over a selection the way typing over one does. 39.31's hidden terminal field is untouched: there Return is what is being sent, and the `↵` is the key that sends it.
- Regression tests: a break lands at the caret, goes over a selection, and appends in a field that was never focused; a capped field is `minLines` 1, `maxLines` 6, text keyboard, done action; the capture line is taller at six lines than at one and no taller at twelve than at six; the newline glyph on the capture strip writes the break into the capture's own controller, and the one on the quick view's strip into the title being edited; the quick view's editor is no longer unbounded.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/design/components/dazzle_field.dart`, `lib/design/components/keyboard_toolbar.dart`, `lib/design/components/field_panel.dart`, `lib/design/components/action_sheet.dart`, `lib/features/home/capture_bar.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/item_sheet.dart`, `lib/features/tasks/item_pickers.dart`, `lib/features/projects/project_detail_screen.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_search.dart`, `test/dazzle_field_test.dart`, `test/capture_bar_test.dart`, `test/item_sheet_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+90.

### 39.92 The jumper is removed (user, 2026-09-08: "but pls, smart user input nav should work for both", "if we cannot get 100% we 1000% rm it")
- **Half of the panes could never be walked, and the half that could was the half that did not need it.** 39.91 scanned whichever buffer the pane was showing, which is the whole history on a classic renderer and exactly one screen on a fullscreen one. On the renderer this user runs, the turns above the top row are in no buffer the app holds: an alternate screen keeps no scrollback, so the text is not late, it is not there. Nothing the app is allowed to do reaches it — writing scroll reports at the pane was struck by 39.82, reading the host's own session files was struck with it, and herdr's own scrollback capture scrolls the pane and refuses while the agent is working, which is when a phone is watching. So the cell offered a walk through the last screen and called it the user's turns. A feature that is right on one renderer and quietly partial on the other is worse than no feature, because the user cannot see which one they got.
- **Everything it was built from goes with it.** `shell_turns.dart` and its suite, the jumper line, the `PATTERN`/`YOUR TURNS` sheet, the bar's `jumping` flag, and `turnMark`/`turnMarkAt` on `AgentKind` — the marks existed for this and for nothing else. 39.82 and 39.91 are struck out of this document rather than left standing as a contract nothing meets.
- **`FIND` is one cell and one line again.** A tap raises the pattern search, a second tap takes it down, and no sheet stands between them. What `FIND` reads is what it always read: the buffer the pane is showing, hits washed in the accent, `PREV` and `NEXT` stepping them. That is honest under either renderer — a search says how many it found, and a count of zero on a screen with no scrollback is an answer, where a jumper that steps three turns out of thirty is a claim.
- **What it would take to come back.** A complete list of the user's turns exists only in the agent's own transcript on the host, and that is a picker: it can name what was said and cannot move a pane that keeps no history to move to. If a pane the app opens is ever known to be on a classic renderer, the scan is a small file to write again — but the app does not set that renderer (39.91's struck ruling holds: it is the user's setting on the user's host), so it is not something the app can arrange for itself.
- Regression tests: the bar draws one cell for finding and none for turns; a tap raises the search line and a second takes it down, with no sheet either way; the search line still counts, steps and washes its hits; neither agent kind carries a turn mark.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/application/agent_kind.dart`, `lib/features/shell/shell_bar.dart`, `lib/features/shell/host_shell_screen.dart`, `test/agent_kind_test.dart`, `test/shell_search_test.dart`. Version 1.0.0+92.

### 39.93 A bundle carries the hosts and keys to another phone (user, 2026-09-08: "it is the open source alternative for termius, moshi, etc", "herdr is a popular multiplexer of the ai era and yeah, we have 1dt class support you can write the export i gueds")
- **39.16 is amended.** It struck an export because nobody had asked for one. The user has now asked, in the frame of the apps the product stands in for: a second phone, or the same phone after a reset, comes up on the same hosts with the same keys, and no backend of the app's own is anywhere in that. So the transport is a file the user holds, and the file is the whole of it.
- **The bundle is the hosts, the keys and the paths, nothing else.** Every key row and, with it, the private key out of the keystore — the one thing 39.21 said never leaves the phone, and the one thing without which the other phone cannot dial. Every host with all its columns: the pinned host key, so the second phone trusts what the first already verified (39.60) instead of meeting the host cold, and the VNC password, which is a host secret like the private key is. Every integration switch. Every path with its directory and label, without its project link: projects are not in the bundle, and a link to a project the other phone does not have is a dangling id. Projects, drops, dispatches, shell tabs, screen frames and settings stay out; they are one phone's history and one phone's taste.
- **A passphrase seals it.** Private keys travel in the file, so the file is ciphertext. The passphrase goes through Argon2id (64 MiB, three passes, one lane, a 16-byte random salt) to a 32-byte key, and the JSON payload goes under NaCl's secretbox (XSalsa20-Poly1305, a 24-byte random nonce) with that key. The envelope is JSON, so a curious `cat` says what it is: a marker, a version, the KDF's name and parameters, salt, nonce and box in base64. On the way in the envelope's own parameters drive the derivation, so a later default does not orphan an older file; a memory parameter above 256 MiB is refused rather than obeyed. A wrong passphrase is exactly what a tampered box is — the tag fails — and is named `WRONG PASSPHRASE` on the assumption that the user typed, not that somebody edited. A file without the marker is `NOT A BUNDLE`; a version the app does not know is `NEWER BUNDLE`. The derivation runs off the UI isolate: the phone reads `SEALING` or `OPENING` on the bar for the second or two it takes.
- **The file goes through the system's document picker.** Nothing of the app's decides where it lives: `EXPORT` raises Android's create-document picker with a name of `dazzle-<date>.bundle`, `IMPORT` its open-document picker, and the bytes go through the device channel, which grows `saveDocument` and `openDocument` for this. The picker dismissed is nothing happening and no line for it. A platform without the picker — the desktop dev loop, the test harness — says `NO PICKER` and stops.
- **On `SYS`, between the alerts and the build stamp** (user, 2026-09-08: "move it to SYS"; the first draft closed the `HOSTS` tab with it, and the user moved it the same day: the bundle is the phone's whole set leaving or arriving, a system act, not a row beside the hosts). A `[ BUNDLE ]` panel with two text actions, `EXPORT` and `IMPORT`. `EXPORT` asks the passphrase twice on a sheet titled `EXPORT BUNDLE`, two obscured fields under `[ PASSPHRASE ]` and `[ AGAIN ]`; the bar names what is missing the way the host form does — `PASSPHRASE MISSING`, `PASSPHRASES DIFFER` — and reads `SEAL` when nothing is. `IMPORT` opens the picker first, then asks the passphrase once on `IMPORT BUNDLE`, and only after the box opens raises a confirm that counts what is in it — `2 HOSTS · 1 KEY · 5 PATHS` — with `IMPORT` filled in the accent, so the user sees what they are about to take before anything is written. The panel keeps the last outcome as a row: `EXPORTED 2 HOSTS · 1 KEY · 5 PATHS`, `IMPORTED …`, or the failure word in the danger colour.
- **Import merges by id and deletes nothing.** A key, host or path the phone already has under the same id is replaced by the bundle's; one it does not have is added; one the phone has and the bundle does not is left alone. A path already on the phone keeps its project link through the replacement, since the bundle carries none. A host's integrations become the bundle's set for that host. A host in the bundle whose key is neither in the bundle nor on the phone is a host that could never dial, and the whole import is refused as `BUNDLE INCOMPLETE` rather than landing half. Private keys are written to the keystore first, then the rows in one transaction: a private key with no row is inert, a row with no private key is a host that cannot dial. Running the same file in twice is the same phone as running it once.
- Regression tests: seal then open with the same passphrase is the payload; a wrong passphrase, a flipped byte, a file without the marker and a version above the app's each fail with their own word; the KDF parameters the envelope carries drive the opening and an oversized memory parameter is refused; export gathers every key with its private key, every host with its pin and VNC password, every integration and every path without its project; import into an empty phone writes all of it, the private keys reaching the keystore; a second import changes nothing; a path already on the phone keeps its project through an import; a host row is replaced by the bundle's under the same id; a bundle whose host names a key nobody has is refused whole and writes nothing; on `SYS` the `[ BUNDLE ]` panel offers `EXPORT` and `IMPORT`, the export sheet names a missing or differing passphrase and seals on a match, the sealed bytes reach the picker channel, an import walks picker, passphrase, count and confirm, and the outcome row reads what happened; a platform without the picker reads `NO PICKER`.
- Ownership: one session; `lib/platform/bundle_cipher.dart`, `lib/platform/documents.dart`, `lib/application/bundle.dart`, `lib/application/reason_lines.dart`, `lib/data/repositories/path_repository.dart`, `lib/design/components/panel_text_action.dart`, `lib/features/hosts/hosts_screen.dart`, `lib/features/settings/settings_screen.dart`, `lib/features/settings/bundle_panel.dart`, `android/app/src/main/kotlin/dev/luisnquin/dazzle/MainActivity.kt`, `test/bundle_cipher_test.dart`, `test/bundle_transfer_test.dart`, `test/bundle_panel_test.dart`, `test/support/platform_channels.dart`, `README.md`. Version 1.0.0+95.

## Shell

### A shell that lost its network comes back on the pane it was on

A link that dies under a live shell puts the tab on six waits — one, two, five, ten, twenty, thirty seconds — and every dial goes back to the same tab, the same terminal and the same attach command against the same pane id, so the agent is where it was and `-- reconnected --` is the only new line between the dead shell's output and the new one's.

The end of that schedule is a tab waiting, not a tab that failed. The link is gone, the terminal still holds every byte the dead shell printed, the tab still carries the command it runs and the pane that command attaches to, and nothing is on a clock: sixty-eight seconds of schedule can run in a pocket, so a phone that was asleep comes back to a shell it can still have rather than to a dead tab. A waiting tab carries what its last dial hit rather than the drop that started the schedule, so the row under the header reads `rose unreachable` and `STOP` ends the tab on those same words. Only a closed stream and a machine that did not answer get there. A key the host refused, a shell it would not give and a host key that is not the one pinned are answers rather than accidents: they fail on the first dial and are never waited on or woken.

Two moments dial a waiting tab again, and it has no clock of its own. The app coming back in front, and a network arriving under it — the platform's default-network callback, so a handover straight from wifi to cell counts as an arrival even though the phone was never without a network, and nothing is replayed on subscribe. Either moment dials every waiting tab once as the first step of a fresh schedule, so a dial that misses waits two seconds and goes on rather than running the whole minute out again unwatched. A tab still inside its schedule has a timer already and is left to it. A tab that failed on an answer is not woken: a new network does not change what the host said.

Nothing dials while the user is looking at `AUTHORIZATION REQUIRED` or at an unknown host key. Answering what the host asked is the user's turn, and a reconnect underneath it would take the shell the question belongs to out from under the answer. The hold is a count, taken when a sheet goes up and released when it comes down however it comes down, so two sheets at once still leave one hold standing.

The middle of the header reads `WAITING`, muted, where `RECONNECTING 4/6` reads, with `STOP` beside it. There is no spinner: the app is not working, it is waiting, and a spinner would say the opposite of both.

### The diff is one viewer, reached from the terminal's header and from the workspace

The header over every terminal carries a version-control mark between the tab strip and the state word — a branch, in the house's own icons, because a row that already holds nine tabs cannot spend four letters on a word. A tap opens the diff on the repository the tab is standing in, so a shell that has just been worked in is one tap from what that work changed. Standing anywhere that is not a checkout, the mark is dimmed and answers nothing: a phone that says `NOT A CHECKOUT` after a tap has spent the tap saying what it already knew.

Which repository that is: the one the shell is standing in, and the app finds that out for itself. Every tab's shell is marked with a variable of its own as it is dialled, and the mark survives the `exec` into the login shell, so the host can be asked which of its processes this tab is. The tap asks — on a second channel of the connection the tab already holds, which the shell on the pty never sees — and reads that process's own working directory off the host. A `cd` typed a second ago is already in the answer, because the answer is the shell's directory and not a copy of it.

The topmost marked process is the tab's shell: everything it starts inherits the mark, and the directory a `vim` is standing in is not the directory the shell is standing in.

A tab that holds the multiplexer is asked of the multiplexer instead, and asked first. What such a tab runs is a client — one pane's attach, or the whole session — and a client stands where it was started for as long as it lives; the shell that has been walked around in belongs to the session on the host, and is no relation of anything this connection started. The same walk finds it: a marked process running the multiplexer names the session it is on, in its environment or on its own command line. A tab attached to one pane is asked for that pane; a tab holding a session is asked for the pane that session has focused, which is the pane the user is looking at. Either way what is taken is the pane shell's own directory and not the foreground program's, for the reason a `vim` is left out above.

The directory is then asked for its repository rather than read as one. The checkout it is inside is what the viewer opens, so a shell three directories deep opens the whole repository instead of a corner of it, and a shell inside no checkout at all is what leaves the mark dimmed.

Three things under it, in order, for a shell that cannot be found — a host that does not keep its processes where Linux keeps them, a tab dialled before it was marked, a connection that has dropped. The escape sequence a shell emits beside its prompt, `file://<host>/<path>`, for a host that has that configured; then the directory the tab was opened at; then the registered directories on that host, one opening straight away and several a pick. The cell is on the row whenever any of them can answer, and a live tab always can: a shell is always standing somewhere.

Struck: asking the user which directory a terminal is in. The terminal knows and the host knows, and a phone that has to be told is not offering much.

**The viewer is a sheet.** The app's modal shape — a panel parked at the bottom of the screen over a dimmed terminal — and not a screen of its own: what is behind it is the context the diff is being read against, and a diff read on a phone is read once and dismissed. It carries no `BACK` and no `REFRESH`. Out is the scrim or the phone's own back, which steps out of a file before it steps out of the sheet, and what is on it was read when it opened. The workspace's change list opens the same sheet at the file that was tapped. There is no second diff surface in the app.

**It is on the screen before the answer is.** The tap opens the sheet, not the round trip that fills it. The mark was lit by a checkout the app had already asked for, so there is nothing left to find out before the sheet can come up; what it does not have yet it says it does not have, `loading` where the subject goes and on the strip of every list, and it fills in underneath as the host answers. A modal that waits on a phone's radio before it draws is a tap that did nothing, and the user taps again.

**Both lists open folded.** `CHANGES` and `COMMITS` are a header strip each when the sheet comes up, each carrying its own count, and the sheet is only as tall as what is unfolded. A diff on a phone is read one file at a time, and two lists of rows over it are the wall between the user and the thing they opened. Unfolding one leaves the other where it is, and picking a file folds both again: which of the three is worth the screen is the user's to say, and the sheet says nothing about it until they have.

**Two panes.** A list beside the hunks, two fifths and the rest: folded with nothing picked the list is the screen, folded with a file picked the hunks are, unfolded both are drawn. One tree at every width, so unfolding the phone never throws away the commit the user had walked to.

**The list is `COMMITS` over `CHANGES`.** The commits are `git log` — the short sha, the subject, the author and how long ago — and the working tree sits above them as a row of its own, which is the row the viewer opens on. Tapping a commit points the whole screen at it: the `CHANGES` panel becomes that commit's files and the hunks become that commit's hunks. The working-tree row carries the dirty count, a commit row carries nothing, and whichever is the source is drawn in `accent`.

**The head line.** Over both panes: the subject of the commit on screen, or `WORKING TREE` when that is the source, then the branch, the short sha and `+n −n` across every file of the source. It is the line the eye lands on and it says what is being read.

**The hunks are coloured by syntax, under the diff's own two colours.** The ground and the glyph still say which side of a change a line is on; the syntax colours the text inside it and never the ground. The language comes from the file's extension and from nothing else, and a line is read on its own: a hunk is not a file, the lines above a line may not be the lines above it in the file, and a highlighter that carries a guess across a hunk boundary is a highlighter that lies. What it cannot place it leaves the colour the line already had, which is the colour the whole line used to be.
